Convergence to equilibrium for cross diffusion systems with nonlocal interaction Thanks: This research was supported by the DFG Collaborative Research Center TRR 109, “Discretization in Geometry and Dynamics”.
Daniel Matthes, Christian Parsch

Abstract
. We study the existence and the rate of equilibration of weak solutions to a two-component system of non-linear diffusion-aggregation equations, with small cross diffusion effects. The aggregation term is assumed to be purely attractive, and in the absence of cross diffusion, the flow is exponentially contractive towards a compactly supported steady state. Our main result is that for small cross diffusion, the system still converges, at a slightly lower rate, to a deformed but still compactly supported steady state. Our approach relies on the interpretation of the PDE system as a gradient flow in a two-component Wasserstein metric. The energy consists of a uniformly convex part responsible for self-diffusion and non-local aggregation, and a totally non-convex part that generates cross diffusion; the latter is scaled by a coupling parameter ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . The core idea of the proof is to perform an ε \varepsilon -dependent modification of the convex/non-convex splitting and establish a control on the non-convex terms by the convex ones.



1.
Introduction

In this paper, we consider the following system of diffusion-aggregation equations with (small) cross diffusion on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
:

(1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho_{1}=\mathrm{div}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{1}\nabla[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+K*\rho_{2}]}}\right),\\
\partial_{t}\rho_{2}=\mathrm{div}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{2}\nabla[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+K*\rho_{1}]}}\right).\end{cases}

Above, the pair
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
of time-dependent probability densities is sought,
F_{1},\,F_{2}
are given non-linearities for the (self-)diffusions,
h
induces cross diffusion of strength
\varepsilon\geq 0
, and
K:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is an attractive interaction kernel. We shall assume that
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are convex and degenerate at zero,
F_{1}^{\prime}(0)=F_{2}^{\prime}(0)=0
, that the derivatives of
h
can be controlled by those of
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
in a way precised further below, and that
K
’s convexity is uniform,
\nabla^{2}K\geq\lambda\,\mathbf{1}
with some
\lambda>0
. The system is augmented with initial conditions,

(2)

\displaystyle\rho_{1}(0,\cdot)=\rho_{1}^{0},\quad\rho_{2}(0,\cdot)=\rho_{2}^{0},

which we assume to have finite energy, see below. The PDE system (
1
) falls into a general class of macroscopic models for multi-species many-particle systems in which long-range attraction forces compete with local diffusion — the former tend to accumulate particles, the latter prevent overcrowding. Such competition is fundamental in various models for interacting particles, including chemotaxis
[
6
,
14
]
, granular media
[
7
,
12
]
, social dynamics
[
3
,
5
]
, semi-conductors
[
15
]
and various further applications. Here, we shall not focus on specific choices of the parameters
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
,
K
and
h
related to any of these models, but consider (
1
) under quite general hypotheses.

1.1.
Motivation: persistence of equilibration

Our goal is to show at the example of the system (
1
) the persistence of the equilibration behaviour in contractive aggregation-(self-)diffusion flows under the influence of cross diffusion. Specifically, we perform a rigorous analysis of existence and long-time asymptotics of weak solutions for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, and prove that the solutions are global in time and converge to the unique equilibrium at almost the same exponential rate as in the unperturbed situation
\varepsilon=0
. This result is beyond perturbation theory not only because of its global character, but also because the perturbation is singular as we shall explain next.

The point of departure for our analysis is the gradient flow formulation of (
1
): the respective energy functional is

(3)

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[F_{1}(\rho_{1})+F_{2}(\rho_{2})+\varepsilon h(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+\rho_{1}K*\rho_{2}]\,\mathrm{d}x,

the metric is a combined
L^{2}
-Wasserstein distance for
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
. In the absence of cross diffusion, i.e., for
\varepsilon=0
, that functional is uniformly geodesically convex, and — thanks to the general theory of metric gradient flows
[
2
]
— the generated gradient flow (
1
) is uniformly exponentially contractive. In particular, there is a unique stationary solution
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
to (
1
), and that is approached at an exponential rate
\lambda>0
, see e.g.
[
2
, Chapter 11]
. Now, the perturbation induced by cross diffusion is singular in the sense that for any
\varepsilon>0
, the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
is not even semi-convex anymore, and consequently, the gradient flow (
1
) is not contractive. That devastating effect of cross diffusion has been proven in a related situation in
[
4
, Prop. 2.4]
.

Despite the failure of the abstract machinery, we shall prove that for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, the equilibration behaviour persists in the sense that there is a unique stationary solution
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\varepsilon}
to (
1
), which is a deformation of
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
above, and that arbitrary solutions converge to
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\varepsilon}
at an exponential rate
\lambda_{\varepsilon}\geq\lambda-C_{0}\varepsilon
. Our result thus contributes to a line of studies on the exponential equilibration in non-convex metric gradient flows, see
[
1
,
4
,
16
,
13
,
17
,
25
,
22
,
23
]
. Differently from these previous works, the confining force leading to equilibration in the unperturbed system (
1
) is not induced by external potentials, but is self-induced via the non-local interaction of the species. This is very natural from a modeling point of view, but induces additional difficulties both on the technical and on the conceptual level. Generally speaking, the equilibrating force of non-local interactions is more fragile than that of fixed external potentials, and requires a more refined analysis. Most prominently, while the integral
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
is a uniformly geodesically convex functional of the pair
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
in the considered metric
[
24
]
, this functional is
not
convex in the sense of affine interpolation, which leads to non-convexity of the entire energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
. Thus we are, for instance, not able to exclude the emergence of additional critical points in the perturbed energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
a priori by standard method from the calculus of variations, but need to obtain the uniqueness of the stationary solution
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\varepsilon}
a posteriori from the global equilibration estimates.

There has been a great recent interest in understanding the long-time behaviour of multi-species cross-diffusion systems, and particularly those with non-local effects. The focus has been on proving qualitative properties of the steady states, in particular segregation phenomena, see
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
and references therein for analytical and numerical studies. It appears, however, that there are no rigorous results on the global exponential stability of these stationary solutions, not even in the non-segregated case that we study here.

1.2.
Main result

Below, we summarize the main results of this paper in Theorem
1.1
. For a concise statement, introduce the space
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
of probability measures on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with finite second moment, and the following subspace
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
of pairs of measures whose combined center of mass is at the origin:

(4)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}:\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x=0}}\right\}.

We prove:

Theorem 1.1
.

Assuming that
F_{1},F_{2},h
and
K
satisfy Hypotheses
2.1
and
2.2
further below, there exists some
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
, the following is true.

•

The energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
possesses a unique
\varepsilon
-dependent global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}=(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})
in
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. The densities
\bar{\rho}_{1}
and
\bar{\rho}_{2}
are continuous in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, radially symmetric about the origin, and compactly supported. They satisfy, with suitable constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, the Euler-Lagrange system

(5)

\begin{split}F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C_{1}-K*\bar{\rho}_{2}}}\right)_{+}\\
F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C_{2}-K*\bar{\rho}_{1}}}\right)_{+}\,.\end{split}

•

Given initial data
\bm{\rho}^{0}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
of finite energy, i.e.
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})<+\infty
, there exists a weakly continuous curve
\bm{\rho}:[0,+\infty)\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}

with
\bm{\rho}(0)=\bm{\rho}^{0}
that satisfies the system (
1
) in the sense that for every compactly supported test function
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
,

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}\,\partial_{t}\zeta-\rho_{1}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+K\ast\rho_{2}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t&=0,\\
\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{2}\,\partial_{t}\zeta-\rho_{2}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+K\ast\rho_{1}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t&=0.\end{split}

•

There is a constant
C_{0}>0
, independent of
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
, such that the energy decays exponentially fast along this solution
\bm{\rho}
with rate
\lambda-C_{0}\varepsilon
:

(6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}(t))-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\leq C_{1}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)\,e^{-2(\lambda-C_{0}\varepsilon)t}

at every
t\geq 0
for some constant
C_{1}
independent of
\varepsilon
and
\bm{\rho}^{0}
. Further,
\bm{\rho}
converges to the unique global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
exponentially in
L^{1}
with the same rate.

1.3.
Strategy of proof

We give cartoon of our approach to proving the persistence of long-time asymptotics in ODE language. Assume that two potentials
E_{0},\,H:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are given, where
E_{0}\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
is uniformly convex of modulus
\lambda>0
, i.e.,
E_{0}^{\prime\prime}\geq\lambda
, and
H\in C^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
is bounded below but
not
convex. For
\varepsilon\geq 0
, define the energy
E_{\varepsilon}:=E_{0}+\varepsilon H
, which is in general not convex for any
\varepsilon>0
. We wish to study the long-time asymptotics of the associated gradient flow,

(7)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=-E_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)=-E_{0}^{\prime}(x)-\varepsilon H^{\prime}(x).

Thanks to the lower bound on
H
, there exists a global minimizer
\bar{x}_{\varepsilon}
of
E_{\varepsilon}
for every
\varepsilon\geq 0
; a priori,
\bar{x}_{\varepsilon}
need not be unique. Now introduce the two auxiliary functions

\displaystyle L_{\varepsilon}(x)=E_{0}(x)+\varepsilon(x-\bar{x}_{\varepsilon})H^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{\varepsilon}),\quad N_{\varepsilon}(x)=H(x)-(x-\bar{x}_{\varepsilon})H^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{\varepsilon}),

and note that
\bar{x}_{\varepsilon}
is the global minimum of the
\lambda
-convex function
L_{\varepsilon}
, and is a critical point of the non-convex function
N_{\varepsilon}
. Finally, we add our core hypothesis on the relation between
H
and
E_{0}
: for
0<\varepsilon<1
, the following inequality holds for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
:

(8)

\displaystyle|N_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)|\leq|L_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)|.

With that at hand,
L_{\varepsilon}
becomes a Lyapunov functional: for any solution
x
to (
7
),

\displaystyle-\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big[L_{\varepsilon}(x)-L_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})\big]=-L_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)\dot{x}

\displaystyle=L_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)^{2}+\varepsilon L_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)N_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\varepsilon)L_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)^{2}\geq 2\lambda(1-\varepsilon)\big[L_{\varepsilon}(x)-L_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})\big],

the last inequality being a consequence of
L_{\varepsilon}
’s
\lambda
-convexity. It follows that
L_{\varepsilon}(x)
tends to its minimal value at the (diminished) exponential rate
2\lambda(1-\varepsilon)
. It then further follows easily that
E_{\varepsilon}(x)
decays at the same exponential rate, and also that the solution
x
itself goes to
\bar{x}_{\varepsilon}
at that rate. And in particular, the chosen minimizer
\bar{x}_{\varepsilon}
turns out to be the unique one.

For (
1
), the roles of
E_{\varepsilon}
and of
H
are played by
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
, which is
\lambda
-uniformly geodesically convex for
\varepsilon=0
, and by the integral of
h(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
, that has no geodesic convexity, respectively. The auxiliary functionals
L_{\varepsilon}
and
N_{\varepsilon}
correspond to, respectively,

(9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}(\rho_{1})+F_{2}(\rho_{2})+\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}+\varepsilon(\rho_{1}V_{1}+\rho_{2}V_{2})}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x

(10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[h(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})-(\rho_{1}V_{1}+\rho_{2}V_{2})}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x,

where the auxiliary potentials
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are chosen to make a global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
, which we obtain via the direct methods, the unique global minimizer of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
, i.e.,

(11)

\displaystyle V_{1}:=\partial_{r_{1}}h\big(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2}),\quad V_{2}:=\partial_{r_{2}}h\big(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2}).

In contrast to
L_{\varepsilon}
in the ODE cartoon above, the modulus of convexity of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is not precisely
\lambda
, but
\lambda-\varepsilon K_{0}
, where
K_{0}
is an upper bound on the second derivatives of
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
, see Corollary
5.2
. Finally, the core relation (
8
) is a functional inequality, see Proposition
5.7
.

1.4.
Outline

We start in Section
2
by specifying the assumptions on the nonlinearities
F_{j}
, the coupling term
h
and the interaction potential
K
. A generic example of functions which satisfy all assumptions is given in the Appendix.
In Section
3
, we prove existence of a global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
of the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
over
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
by means of the direct methods from the calculus of variations, derive the Euler-Lagrange system, and obtain additional regularity properties. In Section
4
, we analyze the time-discrete minimizing movement scheme for the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
, which is then used to construct a weak solution to (
1
)–(
2
). Finally, in Section
5
, we analyze the long-time behaviour of this weak solution and prove exponential convergence to the unique equilibrium for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
.

2.
Preliminaries and hypotheses

2.1.
Notations and definitions

Recall that
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
is the space of probability measures with finite second moment on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
; we consider
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
with the topology induced by narrow convergence. By abuse of notation, we do not distinguish between an absolutely continuous element
\rho\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
and its (Lebesgue-)density
\rho\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. The
L^{2}
-Wasserstein distance
{\mathbf{W}}
is a metric on
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and makes
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
a complete metric space. Convergence in
{\mathbf{W}}
is equivalent to weak convergence of measures and convergence of the second moment. For an introduction to Wasserstein metrics and the related gradient flows, we refer to
[
20
,
21
]
. From the Wasserstein metric on
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, we directly obtain a metric
\mathrm{d}
on the space
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
of pairs
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
of measures,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{\rho},\bm{\eta}}}\right)^{2}:={\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1},\eta_{1})^{2}+{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{2},\eta_{2})^{2}.

The energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}:{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{+\infty\}
formally introduced in (
3
) is defined on pairs
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
with the interpretation that
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})=+\infty
unless
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are both absolutely continuous, and the integral expression is in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
; we shall see below that this definition makes
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
weakly lower semi-continuous, at least for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. For brevity, introduce
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
by
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}}):=F_{1}(r_{1})+F_{2}(r_{2})+\varepsilon h({\mathbf{r}})
, which allows to rewrite
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})+\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}]\,\mathrm{d}x.

The functional
\bm{\rho}\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
will be called the interaction energy.

Next, recall the definition (
4
) of the set
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
of pairs
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
with combined center of mass at zero,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}]=0\quad\text{where}\quad{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{j}]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x\,\rho_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Since
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
is invariant under translations, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\sigma_{v}\bm{\rho})\quad\text{with}\ \sigma_{v}\bm{\rho}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{1}(\cdot+v),\rho_{2}(\cdot+v)}}\right)\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})

for any translation
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and any
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and since (
1
) conserves the combined center of mass, there is no loss of generality to restrict attention to initial data (
2
) and solutions to (
1
) belonging to
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
.

Finally, we introduce the notation
j^{\prime}=3-j
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, so that
\rho_{1^{\prime}}=\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{2^{\prime}}=\rho_{1}
.

2.2.
Summary of hypotheses

Throughout this paper, we consider
F_{1},F_{2},h
and
K
fullfilling the following assumptions:

Hypothesis 2.1
.

Assumptions on the interaction potential
K:{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
:

•

K
is radially symmetric about the origin and it holds
K(0)=0
.

•

K
is
\lambda
-convex with some modulus
\lambda>0
.

•

It holds
K\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and there exists a constant
C_{K}<+\infty
with
\|\nabla^{2}K(z)\|\leq C_{K}
for every
z\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.

Assumptions on the nonlinearities
F_{1},F_{2}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
and coupling term
h:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
:

•

F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are
C^{2}
and strictly convex with
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)>0
for every
r>0
and
j=1,2
.

•

It holds
F_{1}(0)=F_{2}(0)=F_{1}^{\prime}(0)=F_{2}^{\prime}(0)=0
.

•

Their derivatives
F_{1}^{\prime}
and
F_{2}^{\prime}
grow at least linearly for large inputs, but not faster than
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, respectively. More precisely, it holds for
j=1,2
:

(12)

\liminf_{r\to+\infty}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)>0,\quad\limsup_{r\to+\infty}\frac{F_{j}^{\prime}(r)}{F_{j}(r)}<+\infty

•

For small inputs, their second derivatives behave similarly to powers of
r
. Explicitly, for
j=1,2
there exist constants
m_{j},M_{j}>0
, exponents
\beta_{j}\geq 0
and an
r_{0}>0
such that for all
0\leq r\leq r_{0}
, it holds

(13)

m_{j}r^{\beta_{j}}\leq F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)\leq M_{j}r^{\beta_{j}}.

•

They satisfy the McCann-condition for displacement convexity: For all
r\geq 0
,

(14)

F_{j}(r)-rF_{j}^{\prime}(r)+r^{2}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)\geq 0.

•

The coupling function
h
is
C^{2}
and satisfies
h(r_{1},0)=h(0,r_{2})=\partial_{r_{j}}h(r_{1},0)=\partial_{r_{j}}h(0,r_{2})=0
for
j=1,2
and every
r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0
.

It directly follows from the convexity of
F_{j}
that the functions
F_{j}^{\prime}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
are strictly increasing and thus invertible, since they vanish at
0
and grow at least linearly. We can thus introduce the following notations: For
j=1,2
and
\bm{u}=(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, we define

\theta_{j}(\bm{u}):=\partial_{r_{j}}h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((F_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{1}),(F_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{2})}}\right).

In other words,
\theta_{j}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is such that
\theta_{j}(F_{1}^{\prime}(r_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(r_{2}))=\partial_{r_{j}}h({\mathbf{r}})
for all
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
.
Further, we define for
i,j=1,2
the functions
\theta_{j,i}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u}):=\partial_{u_{i}}\theta_{j}(\bm{u})=\frac{\partial_{r_{i}}\partial_{r_{j}}h({\mathbf{r}})}{F_{i}^{\prime\prime}(r_{i})},

where
{\mathbf{r}}=(r_{1},r_{2})=((F_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{1}),(F_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{2}))
. In addition to Hypothesis
2.1
, we impose the following conditions on the
\theta_{j,i}
:

Hypothesis 2.2
.

We assume that the
F_{j}
and
h
are such that

•

The
\theta_{j,i}
are locally Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
.

•

There are constants
\kappa_{j,i}>0
such that

(15)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u})}}\right|\leq\kappa_{j,i}\,\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,u_{1},u_{2},\sqrt{\frac{(F_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{i})}{(F_{j}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{j})}}}}\right\}

for all
\bm{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, which can also be stated in terms of
F_{1},F_{2}
and
h
as

(16)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\partial_{r_{i}}\partial_{r_{j}}h({\mathbf{r}})}}\right|\leq\kappa_{j,i}\,F_{i}^{\prime\prime}(r_{i})\,\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,F_{1}^{\prime}(r_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(r_{2}),\sqrt{\frac{r_{i}}{r_{j}}}}}\right\}

for all
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
.

Example 2.3
.

A simple example for an interaction potential
K
that satisfies Hypothesis
2.1
is given by the quadratic potential
K=\frac{\lambda}{2}|\cdot|^{2}
with some
\lambda>0
. An example for
F_{1},F_{2}
and
h
that fulfill Hypotheses
2.1
and
2.2
is given by Proposition
A.1
in the appendix. Note that the assumptions on
K
are independent from those on
F_{1},F_{2}
and
h
.

2.3.
Basic implications of the hypotheses

We assume in all of the following that
F_{1},F_{2},h
and
K
are such that Hypotheses
2.1
and
2.2
are satisfied. We start by investigating the assumptions more closely and proving some general consequences of them, which will be useful in our analysis. We first prove some more properties of
F_{j}
and
h
.

Proposition 2.4
.

The following hold:

•

There is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that the function

(17)

{\mathbf{r}}\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})=F_{1}(r_{1})+F_{2}(r_{2})+2\varepsilon h({\mathbf{r}})

is convex on
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
for every
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, strictly if
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
.

•

For all
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
and
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, it holds with the constants
\kappa_{j,i}
from (
15
):

(18)

\frac{1}{2}F_{1}(r_{1})+\frac{1}{2}F_{2}(r_{2})\leq\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\kappa_{1,1}}}\right)F_{1}(r_{1})+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\kappa_{2,2}}}\right)F_{2}(r_{2}).

•

With the constants
r_{0}
and
m_{j},M_{j},\beta_{j}
from (
13
), it holds for
0\leq r\leq r_{0}
:

(19)

\displaystyle\frac{m_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}r^{\beta_{j}+1}

\displaystyle\leq F_{j}^{\prime}(r)\leq\frac{M_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}r^{\beta_{j}+1},

(20)

\displaystyle\frac{m_{j}}{(\beta_{j}+1)(\beta_{j}+2)}r^{\beta_{j}+2}

\displaystyle\leq F_{j}(r)\leq\frac{M_{j}}{(\beta_{j}+1)(\beta_{j}+2)}r^{\beta_{j}+2}.

•

For
j=1,2
, there are constants
\alpha_{j}
such that for all
r\geq 0
, it holds

(21)

F_{j}^{\prime}(r)\leq\alpha_{j}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\{1,r\}+F_{j}(r)}}\right).

•

For any bound
H>0
, there is a constant
\beta_{H}>0
such that for all
\bar{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
with
\bar{r}_{1},\bar{r}_{2}\leq H
, all
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
and any
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, the estimate

(22)

\max\{r_{1},r_{2}\}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u})-\theta_{j,i}(\bar{\bm{u}})}}\right|^{2}\leq\beta_{H}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(r_{1}|\bar{r}_{1})+\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}(r_{2}|\bar{r}_{2})}}\right)

holds, where
\bm{u}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime}(r_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(r_{2})}}\right)
,
\bar{\bm{u}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{r}_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{r}_{2})}}\right)
and
\mathrm{d}_{F}(\cdot|\cdot)
is the
Bregman divergence
of a convex function
F
, given by

\mathrm{d}_{F}(r|\bar{r}):=F(r)-F(\bar{r})-F^{\prime}(\bar{r})(r-\bar{r}).

Proof.

We prove convexity of (
17
) by observing that for every
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, it holds

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})=\begin{pmatrix}F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})+2\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{1}}h({\mathbf{r}})&2\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}\partial_{r_{1}}h({\mathbf{r}})\\
2\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{2}}h({\mathbf{r}})&F_{2}^{\prime\prime}(r_{2})+2\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}\partial_{r_{2}}h({\mathbf{r}})\end{pmatrix}.

We show that for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, this matrix is positive semi-definite at every
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. Indeed, it holds for any
\bm{v}=(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by assumption (
16
) and
2v_{1}v_{2}\geq-v_{1}^{2}-v_{2}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{T}\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})\bm{v}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})+2\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{1}}h({\mathbf{r}})}}\right)v_{1}^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{2}^{\prime\prime}(r_{2})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}\partial_{r_{2}}h({\mathbf{r}})}}\right)v_{2}^{2}+4\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{2}}h({\mathbf{r}})v_{1}v_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-2\varepsilon(\kappa_{1,1}+\kappa_{2,1})}}\right)\,F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})v_{1}^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-2\varepsilon(\kappa_{1,2}+\kappa_{2,2})}}\right)\,F_{2}^{\prime\prime}(r_{2})v_{2}^{2}\geq 0

for every sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
by non-negativity of
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}({\mathbf{r}})
. Hence there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
F_{2\varepsilon_{0}}
is convex on
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. For every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})=\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{0}}F_{2\varepsilon_{0}}({\mathbf{r}})+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{0}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}(r_{1})+F_{2}(r_{2})}}\right)

is strictly convex on
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
by strict convexity of
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, proving the first claim. To show the first inequality in (
18
), notice that
\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}
is non-negative in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, since it is convex and its derivative vanishes at 0. Hence

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})=\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})+F_{1}(r_{1})+F_{2}(r_{2})\geq F_{1}(r_{1})+F_{2}(r_{2}).

The second inequality in (
18
) will follow from the fact that
|\partial^{2}_{r_{i}r_{i}}h({\mathbf{r}})|\leq\kappa_{i,i}F_{i}^{\prime\prime}(r_{i})
for all
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
and
i=1,2
by assumption (
16
). Integrated twice from
0
to
r_{i}
and using the assumption that
h
and its first derivatives vanish on
\partial{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, this yields
|h({\mathbf{r}})|\leq\kappa_{i,i}F_{i}(r_{i})
for all
{\mathbf{r}}
, implying (
18
) by definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
.

Inequalities (
19
) and (
20
) follow directly by integrating (
13
) from 0 to
r
and using that
F_{j}(0)=F_{j}^{\prime}(0)=0
. The proof of (
21
) can be done for
r\leq r_{0}
and
r>r_{0}
separately: For
r\leq r_{0}
, the inequality follows from (
19
) since
\beta_{j}\geq 0
and thus
r^{\beta_{j}+1}
decays at least linearly for small
r
. The case
r>r_{0}
follows from the growth assumption (
12
) and the fact that the right-hand side of (
20
) is bounded away from 0 for
r>r_{0}
.

It remains to prove (
22
). Fix
H>0
and let
{\mathbf{r}},\bar{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
with
\bar{r}_{1},\bar{r}_{2}\leq H
. Define

\displaystyle m_{H}:=\min_{s\geq H,\,j=1,2}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(s),\quad M_{H}:=\max_{s\in[0,3H],\,j=1,2}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(s).

It holds
0<m_{H}\leq M_{H}<+\infty
, by
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)>0
for
r>0
and assumption (
12
).
By symmetry of the assumptions and the claim, we can assume w.l.o.g.
r_{1}\geq r_{2}
. We split the proof into two cases:
r_{1}\geq 3H
and
r_{1}\leq 3H
.

Consider first
r_{1}\geq 3H
. We have
\bar{r}_{1}\leq H
and
r_{1}\geq\frac{r_{1}+H}{2}
, thus it holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(r_{1}|\bar{r}_{1})=\int_{\bar{r}_{1}}^{r_{1}}F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(s)(r_{1}-s)\,\mathrm{d}s\geq\int_{H}^{\frac{r_{1}+H}{2}}F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(s)(r_{1}-s)\,\mathrm{d}s\geq\frac{r_{1}-H}{2}\,m_{H}H

where we have used that
F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(s)\geq m_{H}
and
r_{1}-s\geq r_{1}-\frac{r_{1}+H}{2}\geq H
for every
s\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[H,\frac{r_{1}+H}{2}}}\right]
by definition of
m_{H}
and
r_{1}\geq 3H
, respectively. From (
15
) and
H\leq\frac{r_{1}-H}{2}
, it follows

\displaystyle r_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u})-\theta_{j,i}(\bar{\bm{u}})}}\right|^{2}\leq 4\kappa_{j,i}^{2}r_{1}=4\kappa_{j,i}^{2}(r_{1}-H+H)\leq 6\kappa_{j,i}^{2}(r_{1}-H)\leq\frac{12\kappa_{j,i}^{2}}{m_{H}H}\,\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(r_{1}|\bar{r}_{1}).

In the second case, we have
r_{1}\leq 3H
, and thus also
r_{2}\leq 3H
since we assumed
r_{1}\geq r_{2}
. By the monotonicity of
F_{1}^{\prime}
and
F_{2}^{\prime}
, this implies that
\bm{u},\bar{\bm{u}}\in[0,F_{1}^{\prime}(3H)]\times[0,F_{2}^{\prime}(3H)]
, which is a compact subset of
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. Hence by Hypothesis
2.2
, there is a constant
L_{H}
such that
\theta_{j,i}
is
L_{H}
-Lipschitz on this subset. Thus

\displaystyle r_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u})-\theta_{j,i}(\bar{\bm{u}})}}\right|^{2}\leq 3HL_{H}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((u_{1}-\bar{u}_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}-\bar{u}_{2})^{2}}}\right).

We estimate
(u_{j}-\bar{u}_{j})^{2}
by means of
\mathrm{d}_{F_{j}}(r_{j}|\bar{r}_{j})
: We have

\displaystyle(u_{j}-\bar{u}_{j})^{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{j}^{\prime}(r_{j})-F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{r}_{j})}}\right)^{2}=\int_{\bar{r}_{j}}^{r_{j}}2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{j}^{\prime}(s)-F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{r}_{j})}}\right)F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 2M_{H}\,\mathrm{d}_{F_{j}}(r_{j}|\bar{r}_{j}),

since the term
F_{j}^{\prime}(s)-F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{r}_{j})
is negative if and only if
r_{j}<\bar{r}_{j}
, in which case the endpoints
\bar{r}_{j}
and
r_{j}
of the integral are swapped. This finishes the proof of (
22
).
∎

We additionally prove some important general properties of the energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
and its components. In all of the following, we assume
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
with
\varepsilon_{0}
from Proposition
2.4
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
be any pair and let
\mathcal{S}\subset{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
be any family of pairs.

•

The following estimates hold:

(23)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[F_{1}(\rho_{1})+F_{2}(\rho_{2})]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}),\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}).

In particular, the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})
is uniformly bounded for
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{S}
if and only if both
F_{j}(\rho_{j})
and
\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}
are uniformly bounded in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
for
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{S}
.

•

With the constants
\alpha_{j}
from (
21
), it holds:

(24)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\alpha_{j}\,(1+2\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})).

•

For
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, the interaction energy
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
satisfies the bounds

(25)

\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2}]}}\right)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2}]}}\right).

In particular, for
\mathcal{S}\subset{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
,
\bm{\rho}
’s interaction energy is uniformly bounded for
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{S}
if and only if
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
have uniformly bounded second moments for
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

The interaction energy is clearly non-negative by our assumptions on
K
, thus the first estimate in (
23
) follows directly from (
18
). The second one holds since
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
is a convex function whose derivative vanishes at 0, and thus is non-negative in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. By (
21
), it holds
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j})\leq\alpha_{j}\,(\rho_{j}+F_{j}(\rho_{j}))
. From this we obtain (
24
) by integrating and using (
23
) and the fact that
\rho_{j}
has unit mass.

In order to prove (
25
), note that by
\lambda
-convexity of
K
and the bound on its second derivative, it holds
\frac{\lambda}{2}|z|^{2}\leq K(z)\leq\frac{C_{K}}{2}|z|^{2}
. Since for
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, we have
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}]=-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]
, we thus obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}(x)\rho_{2}(y)K(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}(x)\rho_{2}(y)|x-y|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}(x)\rho_{2}(y)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}-2x\cdot y}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2}]}}\right)-\lambda{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]\cdot{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}]=\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2}]}}\right)+\lambda|{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2}]}}\right).

For the upper bound, the same argument yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{C_{K}}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2}]}}\right)+C_{K}|{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]|^{2}\leq C_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2}]}}\right),

where the last step follows from
|{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(|{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]|^{2}+|{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}]|^{2})\leq\frac{1}{2}({\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2}])
.
∎

In the next section, we shall use these properties to proof existence of a global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
via the direct method from the calculus of variations.

3.
Existence and properties of the minimizer for the energy functional

3.1.
Existence of minimizers

We start by proving the following weak coercivity result for the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
. The proof mainly uses the estimates from Lemma
2.5
.

Lemma 3.1
.

The sublevel sets of the energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
in
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
are pre-compact in the weak
L^{1}
-topology, i.e. any sequence of pairs
(\bm{\rho}^{n})=(\rho_{1}^{n},\rho_{2}^{n})\subset{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
with
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{n})
uniformly bounded contains a subsequence converging weakly in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
to some
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. The same holds true for sequences
(\bm{\rho}^{n})\subset{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
with
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{n})
uniformly bounded, such that their combined center of mass
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}^{n}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}^{n}]
converges to
0
.

Proof.

Take any sequence
(\bm{\rho}^{n})\subset{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
such that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}^{n}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}^{n}]\to 0
and
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{n})
is uniformly bounded. We consider the shifted pair
\tilde{\bm{\rho}}^{n}:=\bm{\rho}^{n}(\cdot-v_{n})
with the shift
v_{n}:=\frac{1}{2}({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}^{n}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}^{n}])
. We have
\tilde{\bm{\rho}}^{n}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, and from the translation invariance of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
, it follows that
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\bm{\rho}}^{n})=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{n})
is uniformly bounded. It thus follows from (
23
) and (
25
) that the second moments
{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{n}],{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\tilde{\rho}_{2}^{n}]
are uniformly bounded. Together with
v_{n}\to 0
, this implies uniform boundedness of the second moments of
\rho_{j}^{n}
:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{j}^{n}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{2}\tilde{\rho}_{j}^{n}(x+v_{n})\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|y-v_{n}|^{2}\tilde{\rho}_{j}^{n}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq 2{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\tilde{\rho}_{j}^{n}]+2|v_{n}|^{2}.

In particular, the sequence
(\rho_{j}^{n})
is tight as probability measures for
j=1,2
, and thus converges narrowly to a
\rho_{j}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
along some subsequence, again denoted
(\rho_{j}^{n})
. Since narrow convergence and uniform boundedness of the second moments imply
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{j}^{n}]\to{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{j}]
, we have
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
.

To prove
\rho_{j}^{n}\rightharpoonup\rho_{j}
weakly in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, observe that by (
23
), the integrals of
F_{j}(\rho_{j}^{n})
are uniformly bounded. Since
F_{j}(r)
grows super-linearly for
r\to+\infty
by the at least linear growth of
F_{j}^{\prime}(r)
, the de la Vallée-Poussin compactness criterion, see e.g.
[
19
, Theorem 1.28]
, yields compactness of the sequence
\rho_{j}^{n}
with respect to local weak
L^{1}
-convergence. Together with the narrow convergence
\rho_{j}^{n}\rightharpoonup\rho_{j}
, this implies
\rho_{j}^{n}\rightharpoonup\rho_{j}
weakly in
L^{1}_{loc}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. Together with tightness, this yields
\rho_{j}^{n}\rightharpoonup\rho_{j}
weakly in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, proving the claim.
∎

In order to apply the direct method in the calculus of variations, we need to prove lower semi-continuity of the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
in the same topology.

Lemma 3.2
.

The energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
is lower semi-continuous in
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
with respect to weak
L^{1}
-convergence.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
is non-negative by (
18
), Fatou’s lemma yields lower semi-continuity of the functional
\bm{\rho}\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})\,\mathrm{d}x
with respect to strong
L^{1}
-convergence. Since
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
is a convex function, this implies weak lower semi-continuity. It remains to show that the interaction energy
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
is lower semi-continuous with respect to weak
L^{1}
-convergence. Let
\bm{\rho}^{n}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
with
\rho_{j}^{n}\rightharpoonup\rho_{j}
weakly in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and for any radius
R>0
, define

\displaystyle K_{R}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{d}}),\ K_{R}(z):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}K(z)&|z|\leq R\\
0&|z|>R\end{array}}}\right..

Also note that
\rho_{j}^{n}\rightharpoonup\rho_{j}
weakly in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
for
j=1,2
implies
\rho_{1}^{n}\otimes\rho_{2}^{n}\rightharpoonup\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}
weakly in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. Since
0\leq K_{R}\leq K
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, this yields

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{n}K\ast\rho_{2}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{n}K_{R}\ast\rho_{2}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}x+\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{n}(K-K_{R})\ast\rho_{2}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{n}\otimes\rho_{2}^{n}(x,y)\,K_{R}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K_{R}\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Since
R
was arbitrary, we can take the limit
R\to\infty
. By the monotone convergence theorem, the integral on the right-hand side converges to
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
, proving weak lower semi-continuity of the interaction energy.
∎

Together, these two lemmas yield existence of a global minimizer of the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
in
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
.

Corollary 3.3
.

There exists a pair
\bar{\bm{\rho}}=(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, depending on
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, that minimizes the energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
over
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
.

Proof.

The existence of
\bar{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
that minimizes
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
over
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
follows from the previous two lemmas via the direct method from the calculus of variations. Since
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
is translation invariant, the minimality of
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
over
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
implies that
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
also minimizes
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})
over the whole space
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Note that we do not obtain uniqueness of the minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
directly, since the energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
is not convex on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, neither along geodesics, because of the coupling term, nor in the flat sense, because of the interaction term. We shall prove uniqueness of the minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
for every sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
in section
5
, see Corollary
5.11
.

3.2.
Euler-Lagrange system

We now use a variational argument to prove that the minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
obtained from Corollary
3.3
satisfies the Euler-Lagrange system (
5
).

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
be compactly supported, and assume that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{2}=0
and
\bar{\rho}_{j}+\psi_{j}\geq 0
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
for
j=1,2
. Additionally, assume that there exist constants
H_{j}>0
such that
\psi_{j}=0
on the set
\{\bar{\rho}_{j}>H_{j}\}
. Then it holds

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}}}\right)\psi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x\,+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}}}\right)\psi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\geq 0.

Proof.

Let
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
as above. Then for any
s\in[0,1]
, the perturbation
\bm{\rho}^{s}:=\bar{\bm{\rho}}+s\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}}\right)
fulfills
\rho^{s}_{j}\geq 0
and
\int\rho^{s}_{j}=1
for
j=1,2
, and thus defines a pair of probability distributions. Since
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
minimizes
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
over
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
, it holds
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})\geq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})
for all
s\in[0,1]
, hence

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}s}}}\right|_{s=0^{+}}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}s}}}\right|_{s=0^{+}}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\rho}_{1}+s\psi_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2}+s\psi_{2})\geq 0\,.

Using the properties of
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
, in particular the fact that they are supported on a set where
\bar{\rho}_{j}
is bounded, the dominated convergence theorem applies and provides that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}s}}}\right|_{s=0^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\rho}_{1}+s\psi_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2}+s\psi_{2})+(\bar{\rho}_{1}+s\psi_{1})K\ast(\bar{\rho}_{2}+s\psi_{2})}}\right]\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\partial_{r_{1}}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}}}\right)\psi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\partial_{r_{2}}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}}}\right)\psi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}}}\right)\psi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}}}\right)\psi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

which proves the asserted integral inequality.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

There are positive constants
C_{1},C_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
satisfies

(26)

\begin{split}F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K*\bar{\rho}_{2}&\geq C_{1}\\
F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K*\bar{\rho}_{1}&\geq C_{2}\end{split}

with equality a.e. on
\{\bar{\rho}_{1}>0\}
and a.e. on
\{\bar{\rho}_{2}>0\}
respectively.

Proof.

For brevity, we introduce the notation

EL_{1}:=F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K*\bar{\rho}_{2},

and similarly
EL_{2}
. Since both inequalities in (
26
) are analogous to each other, we only need to prove the first one. Suppose there does not exist a constant
C_{1}
such that
EL_{1}\equiv C_{1}
a.e on
\{\bar{\rho}_{1}>0\}
and
EL_{1}\geq C_{1}
a.e. on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. Then there exist constants
\alpha<\beta
, a height
H>0
and a radius
R>0
such that the bounded measurable sets

M_{\alpha}:=\{EL_{1}\leq\alpha\}\cap\{\bar{\rho}_{1}\leq H\}\cap B_{R},\qquad M_{\beta}:=\{EL_{1}\geq\beta\}\cap\{0<\bar{\rho}_{1}\leq H\}\cap B_{R}

both have strictly positive measure. Now define

\psi_{1}:=-\bar{\rho}_{1}\mathds{1}_{M_{\beta}}+\frac{1}{|M_{\alpha}|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{M_{\beta}}\bar{\rho}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)\mathds{1}_{M_{\alpha}}.

The properties
EL_{1}\leq\alpha
on
M_{\alpha}
and
EL_{1}\geq\beta
on
M_{\beta}
yield

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}EL_{1}\,\psi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{M_{\beta}}EL_{1}\,\bar{\rho}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{|M_{\alpha}|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{M_{\beta}}\bar{\rho}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)\int_{M_{\alpha}}EL_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq(\alpha-\beta)\int_{M_{\beta}}\bar{\rho}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x<0,

where the last inequality follows from
\bar{\rho}_{1}>0
on
M_{\beta}
.
This is however a contradiction to Lemma
3.5
with
\psi_{2}\equiv 0
, since it is easily checked that
\psi_{1}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
satisfies all properties needed to apply the lemma.

It remains to show that
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are strictly positive. We observe that the strict convexity of
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
implies for
j=1,2
:

(27)

F_{j}^{\prime}(r_{j})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{j}}h({\mathbf{r}})=\partial_{r_{j}}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})\geq 0\quad\text{for all }{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2},

with equality if and only if
r_{j}=0
. The interaction terms
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j}
are strictly positive on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
by positivity of
K
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\setminus\{0\}
and the fact that
\bar{\rho}_{j}
is non-singular. Hence
EL_{j}(x)>0
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and
j=1,2
, and thus
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

For a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, it holds

\displaystyle K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)>C_{1}\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)=0

\displaystyle K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)<C_{1}\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)>0

and the same statements hold true for
\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)\geqq 0
and
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)\lessgtr C_{2}
.

Proof.

If
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)>C_{1}
, then it follows from (
27
) that (
26
) does not hold with equality, hence up to a null-set,
\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)=0
. On the other hand, if
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)<C_{1}
, then (
26
) implies
F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1}(x))+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1}(x),\bar{\rho}_{2}(x))>0
, and thus
\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)>0
.

It follows immediately that
\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)=0
implies
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)\geq C_{1}
and that
\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)>0
implies
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)\leq C_{1}
for a.e.
x
. Since
K
, and thus
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}
, is
\lambda
-convex on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, the set
\{x:K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)=C_{1}\}
has measure zero, which concludes the proof.
∎

From the previous two results, we obtain the Euler-Lagrange system and a characterization of the supports of
\bar{\rho}_{1}
and
\bar{\rho}_{2}
:

Theorem 3.8
.

Any global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
of the energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the Euler-Lagrange system

(28)

\begin{split}F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C_{1}-K*\bar{\rho}_{2}}}\right)_{+}\\
F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C_{2}-K*\bar{\rho}_{1}}}\right)_{+}\end{split}

in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. Moreover, the supports of
\bar{\rho}_{1}
and
\bar{\rho}_{2}
are given by the compact convex sets

(29)

\begin{split}\mathrm{supp}\,\bar{\rho}_{1}&=\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}\leq C_{1}\}\\
\mathrm{supp}\,\bar{\rho}_{2}&=\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}\leq C_{2}\}.\end{split}

Proof.

The form (
28
) of the Euler-Lagrange system follows immediately from Lemmas
3.6
and
3.7
, and the characterization (
29
) of the supports follows from Lemma
3.7
and the observation
\overline{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}<C_{1}\}}=\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}\leq C_{1}\}
, which holds by
\lambda
-convexity of
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}
.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

In the case that
K=\frac{\lambda}{2}|\cdot|^{2}
is the quadratic interaction potential, the Euler-Lagrange system can be written as

(30)

\begin{split}F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})&=(\tilde{C}_{1}-\frac{\lambda}{2}|x|^{2})_{+}\\
F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})&=(\tilde{C}_{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2}|x|^{2})_{+}.\end{split}

with the constants
\tilde{C}_{1},\tilde{C}_{2}>0
chosen uniquely such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x=1
for
j=1,2
. It holds
\tilde{C}_{j}=C_{j}-\frac{\lambda}{2}{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}}]
with the constants
C_{j}
from (
28
); recall that
j^{\prime}:=3-j
. The form (
30
) is considerably simpler to solve than (
28
), since the right-hand side does not explicitly depend on
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
. It can be proven by showing that in the case
K=\frac{\lambda}{2}|\cdot|^{2}
, the energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
is strictly convex in the flat sense on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, implying that the minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
is unique. This uniqueness yields radial symmetry of
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
, see Lemma
5.12
, hence
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{j}]=0
for both components. A direct calculation then yields
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j}=\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|\cdot|^{2}+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\bar{\rho}_{j}]}}\right]
, thus (
30
) follows from (
28
).

3.3.
Properties of the minimizers

In the next results, we prove some additional properties of the minimizers
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For any
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, the minimal energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})
, the constants
C_{1},C_{2}
from the Euler-Lagrange system (
28
) and the diameters of
\mathrm{supp}\,\bar{\rho}_{1}
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\bar{\rho}_{2}
are
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. If
\bar{\varepsilon}<1/\max\{\kappa_{1,1},\kappa_{2,2}\}
, there exists an
\varepsilon
-independent constant
H_{0}>0
such that
\bar{\rho}_{j}\leq H_{0}
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
for
j=1,2
and any
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
.

Proof.

Since for any fixed
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded for small
\varepsilon
, the minimal value
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})
is uniformly bounded as well. To prove the other claims, we write the Euler-Lagrange system using the functions
\theta_{j}
:

(31)

\begin{split}F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon\theta_{1}(F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2}))=(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}\\
F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})+\varepsilon\theta_{2}(F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2}))=(C_{2}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1})_{+}\end{split}

It follows from assumption (
15
) that
|\theta_{j}(\bm{u})|\leq\kappa_{j,i}u_{i}
for all
\bm{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
and any
i,j=1,2
. It thus follows from (
31
) that

(32)

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon\kappa_{1,1})\,F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})\leq(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}\leq(1+\varepsilon\kappa_{1,1})\,F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1}),

and analogously for
F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})
. For the integral of the right-hand side, we have by (
21
)

\displaystyle(1+\varepsilon\kappa_{1,1})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x\leq(1+\varepsilon\kappa_{1,1})\,\alpha_{1}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+2\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right),

which is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. Hence by (
32
), the integral of
(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded as well. With
\bar{x}:=\argmin_{x}K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}
, we have, using the bound
C_{K}
on the Hessian of
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}
and inequality (
23
),

\displaystyle K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)\leq K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(\bar{x})+\frac{C_{K}}{2}|x-\bar{x}|^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{1}K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{C_{K}}{2}|x-\bar{x}|^{2}\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})+\frac{C_{K}}{2}|x-\bar{x}|^{2}

for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. Hence we can estimate the integral of
(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}
from below by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(C_{1}-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})-C_{K}|x-\bar{x}|^{2})_{+}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Since the left-hand side of this estimate is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded, the same is true for the right-hand side, which implies that
C_{1}
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. Since it holds
\frac{\lambda}{2}|x-\bar{x}|^{2}\leq K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}\leq C_{1}
in
\mathrm{supp}\,\bar{\rho}_{1}
, this directly implies that the diameter of
\mathrm{supp}\,\bar{\rho}_{1}
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. If
\bar{\varepsilon}<1/\max\{\kappa_{1,1},\kappa_{2,2}\}
, implying that
1-\varepsilon\kappa_{1,1}
is bounded away from 0 uniformly for
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
, the uniform boundedness of
C_{1}
and non-negativity of
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}
together with (
32
) imply uniform boundedness of
F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})
, and thus also for
\bar{\rho}_{1}
by the growth of
F_{1}^{\prime}
. The proof for the other component is analogous.
∎

In the next lemma, we prove an
\varepsilon
-uniform regularity result for the minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Assuming that
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
is sufficiently small, the functions
F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})
are Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and the gradients
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})
are Lipschitz in the common support
\{\bar{\rho}_{1}>0\}\cap\{\bar{\rho}_{2}>0\}
, both with Lipschitz constants independent of
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
.

Proof.

We consider the function
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, defined by

\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(\bm{u}):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{u}+\varepsilon\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1}(\bm{u})\\
\theta_{2}(\bm{u})\end{pmatrix}=\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathbf{r}}}}\right)&\text{ for }\bm{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\\
\bm{u}&\text{ else}\end{array}}}\right.,

where
{\mathbf{r}}=((F_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{1}),(F_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{2}))
for
\bm{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. It follows from the form (
31
) of the Euler-Lagrange system that

(33)

\Gamma_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})}}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}\\
(C_{2}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1})_{+}\end{pmatrix}.

By strict convexity of
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
, the map
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
is injective. Since both
\theta_{j}
vanish on
\partial{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, we have
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{id}
there, and
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
is continuous in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. To prove that
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
maps
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{2}
to itself surjectively, take any
\bm{v}=(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{2}
and consider the minimizer
\bar{\mathbf{r}}
of the function
{\mathbf{r}}\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})-\bm{v}\cdot{\mathbf{r}}
over
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. The minimizer exists by the superlinear growth of
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
, and it does not lie on the boundary
\partial{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, as for any
{\mathbf{r}}
with
r_{i}=0
, it holds
\partial_{r_{i}}(\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})-\bm{v}\cdot{\mathbf{r}})=-v_{i}<0
. We thus have
\bar{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{2}
, and thus by the necessary first-order minimality condition, it holds
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\mathbf{r}})=\bm{v}
, implying that
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{r}_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{r}_{2}))=\bm{v}
. This shows that
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
maps
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
and
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{2}
onto themselves bijectively, and we can rewrite (
33
) as

(34)

\begin{pmatrix}F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})\\
F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})\end{pmatrix}=\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}\\
(C_{2}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1})_{+}\end{pmatrix}.

We claim that for
\bar{\varepsilon}
sufficiently small, the inverse mapping
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
is of class
C^{1}
, and its Jacobian
{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
is globally bounded and locally Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, uniformly with respect to
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. We prove the claim by the inverse function theorem. In
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{2}
, we have with (
15
)

\displaystyle\det{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(\bm{u})=\det\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1,1}(\bm{u})&\theta_{1,2}(\bm{u})\\
\theta_{2,1}(\bm{u})&\theta_{2,2}(\bm{u})\end{pmatrix}}}\right]\geq 1-\varepsilon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\kappa_{1,1}+\kappa_{2,2}}}\right)-\varepsilon^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\kappa_{1,1}\kappa_{2,2}+\kappa_{1,2}\kappa_{2,1}}}\right),

which is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded away from 0 for
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
, if
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
is sufficiently small.
In
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{2}
, we have
{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{Id}
. Since the
\theta_{j,i}
vanish on
\partial{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
by hypothesis (
15
), and since they are locally Lipschitz,
{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
is locally Lipschitz on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, uniformly for
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. Hence the inverse function theorem is applicable and yields
C^{1}
-regularity of the inverse
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, with Jacobian
{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}\circ\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}}}\right)^{-1}
. Since
\det{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded away from 0 on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, this implies that
D\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, implying that
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
is globally Lipschitz on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with an
\varepsilon
-independent Lipschitz constant for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. Since the inverse mapping
A\mapsto A^{-1}
is Lipschitz on any set on
2\times 2
-matrices
A
with
\det A
bounded away from 0, this also implies that
{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
is locally Lipschitz on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, uniformly in
\varepsilon
, since it is a composition of locally Lipschitz functions. This finishes the proof of the claim.

By Lemma
3.10
, the diameter of
\mathrm{supp}\,\bar{\rho}_{j}
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded. Together with the bound
|\nabla K(z)|\leq C_{K}|z|
, this implies that
(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}
and
(C_{2}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1})_{+}
are globally Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, uniformly in
\varepsilon
. It thus follows from (
34
) and the
\varepsilon
-uniform Lipschitz continuity of
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
that the
F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})
are
\varepsilon
-uniformly Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. On the set
\{\bar{\rho}_{1}>0\}\cap\{\bar{\rho}_{2}>0\}
, it holds
(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}=C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}
and
(C_{2}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1})_{+}=C_{2}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}
by Lemma
3.7
, which are of class
C^{2}
by the regularity of
K
. Thus, their gradients are
\varepsilon
-uniformly Lipschitz in
\{\bar{\rho}_{1}>0\}\cap\{\bar{\rho}_{2}>0\}
, and (
34
) together with the local Lipschitz-continuity of
{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
implies
\varepsilon
-uniform Lipschitz-continuity of
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})
in the common support of
\bar{\rho}_{1}
and
\bar{\rho}_{2}
. Note that local Lipschitz-continuity of
{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
is sufficient, since the constants
C_{1},C_{2}
are
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded by Lemma
3.10
and thus
{\mathrm{D}}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly Lipschitz on the compact set
[0,C_{1}]\times[0,C_{2}]\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

An important consequence of this lemma is the following semiconvexity result, which we shall use in section
5
to prove exponential convergence to the equilibrium for system (
1
).

Corollary 3.12
.

For the same
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
as in the previous lemma, there exists an
\varepsilon
-independent constant
K_{0}>0
such that
V_{j}:=\partial_{r_{j}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})
is
(-K_{0})
-semiconvex in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
for
j=1,2
and every
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
.

Proof.

We show that for some
\varepsilon
-independent constant
K_{0}
, the gradient
\nabla V_{j}
is
K_{0}
-Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
for
j=1,2
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. With
\bar{\bm{u}}:=(F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2}))
, it holds

(35)

\nabla V_{j}=\nabla\theta_{j}(\bar{\bm{u}})=\theta_{j,1}(\bar{\bm{u}})\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\theta_{j,2}(\bar{\bm{u}})\nabla F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2}),

which is nonzero only in
\{\bar{\rho}_{1}>0\}\cap\{\bar{\rho}_{2}>0\}
, since the
\theta_{j,i}
vanish on
\partial{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
by assumption (
15
). Inside the common support of
\bar{\rho}_{1}
and
\bar{\rho}_{2}
, the
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})
are
\varepsilon
-uniformly Lipschitz by Lemma
3.11
. Since by Lemma
3.10
, the
\bar{\rho}_{j}
are
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded by a constant
H_{0}
, the function
\bar{\bm{u}}
only attains values in the compact set
[0,F_{1}^{\prime}(H_{0})]\times[0,F_{2}^{\prime}(H_{0})]
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. By Hypothesis
2.2
, the
\theta_{j,i}
are Lipschitz on this set, and
\bar{\bm{u}}
is globally Lipschitz by Lemma
3.11
, uniformly in
\varepsilon
, hence
\theta_{j,i}(\bar{\bm{u}})
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. This proves that the expression (
35
) is Lipschitz in
\{\bar{\rho}_{1}>0\}\cap\{\bar{\rho}_{2}>0\}
. Additionally, it is continuous across the boundary of the common support. To see this, take any
x^{*}
on the boundary, and any sequence
x_{n}\to x^{*}
with
x_{n}\in\{\bar{\rho}_{1}>0\}\cap\{\bar{\rho}_{2}>0\}
. Since
x^{*}
lies on the boundary and both
F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})
are continuous in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, at least one of the two sequences
\bar{u}_{k}(x_{n})=F_{k}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{k}(x_{n}))
for
k=1,2
converges to 0, implying by (
15
) that
\theta_{j,i}(\bar{\bm{u}}(x_{n}))\to 0
for both
i
. Since the
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}
-terms are bounded along the sequence
(x_{n})
, this implies that the expression (
35
) evaluated at
x_{n}
converges to 0, yielding continuity across the boundary. This proves that the
\nabla V_{j}
are
\varepsilon
-uniformly Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with some Lipschitz constant
K_{0}
, yielding
(-K_{0})
-semiconvexity on
V_{j}
for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
.
∎

4.
Minimizing movement scheme for the energy functional

Fixing any
\tau>0
, we introduce the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}
, defined for
\bm{\rho},\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
as

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}|\hat{\bm{\rho}}):=\frac{1}{2\tau}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}).

Our goal is to get a time-discrete variational approximation for a weak solution of system (
1
) by iteratively minimizing the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
over
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
, with
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
being the minimizing pair obtained in the previous iteration.

4.1.
Existence of minimizers for the modified functional

We start by proving existence of global minimizers of the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
.

Lemma 4.1
.

For every pair
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
, the functional
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}\ni\bm{\rho}\mapsto\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
possesses a global minimizer
\bm{\rho}^{+}
. Every minimizer
\bm{\rho}^{+}
satisfies the inequality

(36)

\frac{1}{2\tau}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}).

If
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, then also
\bm{\rho}^{+}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
for every global minimizer
\bm{\rho}^{+}
.

Proof.

We first claim that for any
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
, the minimization problem of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
over
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
is equivalent to the restricted minimization problem over the set of all
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
which have the same combined center of mass as
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
, i.e.
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}]={\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{2}]
.

To prove the claim, fix any
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
with
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})<+\infty
, so in particular, the
\rho_{j}
for
j=1,2
are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Denote by
\Phi_{j}:{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
convex functions such that
\nabla\Phi_{j}:{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
are optimal transport maps from
\rho_{j}
to
\hat{\rho}_{j}
, which exist by Brenier’s theorem. Now consider the pair of translated densities
\sigma_{v}\bm{\rho}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{1}(\cdot+v),\rho_{2}(\cdot+v)}}\right)
, for any direction
v\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. Clearly, the maps
\nabla\Phi_{j}(\cdot+v)
push
\sigma_{v}\rho_{j}
forward to
\hat{\rho}_{j}
, and since they are the gradients of the convex functions
\Phi_{j}(\cdot+v)
, they are optimal in the transport problem from
\sigma_{v}\rho_{j}
to
\hat{\rho}_{j}
. This implies

\displaystyle{\mathbf{W}}(\sigma_{v}\rho_{j},\hat{\rho}_{j})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla\Phi_{j}(x+v)-x|^{2}\,\rho_{j}(x+v)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla\Phi_{j}(y)-y+v|^{2}\,\rho_{j}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla\Phi_{j}(y)-y|^{2}\,\rho_{j}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y+|v|^{2}+2\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nabla\Phi_{j}(y)-y}}\right)\,\rho_{j}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y}}\right)\cdot v

\displaystyle={\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{j},\hat{\rho}_{j})^{2}+|v|^{2}+2\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{j}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{j}]}}\right)\cdot v\,.

Combining this fact with the translation invariance of the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\sigma_{v}\bm{\rho}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})=\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(\sigma_{v}\rho_{1},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}+{\mathbf{W}}(\sigma_{v}\rho_{2},\hat{\rho}_{2})^{2}}{2\tau}+\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\sigma_{v}\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}+{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{2},\hat{\rho}_{2})^{2}+2|v|^{2}+2\,({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{2}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}])\cdot v}{2\tau}+\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})+\frac{|v|^{2}+\,({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{2}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}])\cdot v}{\tau}\,.

This expression is quadratic in
v\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and is uniquely minimized by
\bar{v}
given by

\displaystyle\bar{v}:=-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{2}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}]}}\right).

For the combined center of mass of this optimally shifted pair
\sigma_{\bar{v}}\bm{\rho}
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\sigma_{\bar{v}}\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\sigma_{\bar{v}}\rho_{2}]={\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}]-2\bar{v}={\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{2}].

This proves the claimed equivalence of the minimization problems over the whole
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
and the restricted set of pairs which preserve
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
’s combined center of mass.

To show existence of a global minimizer, we can thus w.l.o.g. assume
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
and prove existence of a minimizer
\bm{\rho}^{+}
over
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. Similarly as in the previous section, we shall use the direct method from the calculus of variations. Since the distance term is non-negative, pre-compactness of the sublevel sets of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
follows directly from Lemma
3.1
. Weak lower semicontinuity follows from Lemma
3.2
and lower semi-continuity of the Wasserstein distance with respect to weak
L^{1}
-convergence, proving the existence of a minimizer
\bm{\rho}^{+}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
.

From the minimality of
\bm{\rho}^{+}
, we directly obtain the inequality

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\tau}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}}^{+})^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}^{+}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}),

which implies (
36
), thus finishing the proof.
∎

As a first regularity result, we prove a global
L^{2}
-estimate for
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
, restricted to a set where
\rho_{j}^{+}
is smaller than some fixed height
\alpha>0
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
be any fixed pair, and let
\alpha>0
be a constant. Denote by
\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}):=\mathcal{H}(\rho_{1})+\mathcal{H}(\rho_{2}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\rho_{1}\log\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\log\rho_{2}]\,\mathrm{d}x
the combined (negative) entropy functional for
\rho\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and let
[\rho]_{\alpha}:=\min\{\rho,\alpha\}
denote a density
\rho
cut off at height
\alpha
. Then any minimizer
\bm{\rho}^{+}
of the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
satisfies

(37)

\frac{1}{2A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([\rho_{1}^{+}]_{\alpha}}}\right)}}\right|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{2}^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([\rho_{2}^{+}]_{\alpha}}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+2dC_{K}\,,

where
A:=\max_{j=1,2}\max_{r\leq\alpha}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)<+\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\bm{\rho}^{+}
be any minimizer of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
over
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
. We prove (
37
) by analyzing the behaviour of the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
along the heat flow with initial datum
\bm{\rho}^{+}
: Denote by
\mathcal{K}^{s}
the heat kernel in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and let
\rho_{j}^{s}
be the solution of the heat equation
\partial_{s}\rho_{j}^{s}=\Delta\rho_{j}^{s}
for
(s,x)\in[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with initial condition
\rho_{j}^{0}=\rho_{j}^{+}
for
j=1,2
. Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{s}(x)=\frac{1}{(4\pi s)^{d/2}}e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4s}},\qquad\rho_{j}^{s}=\mathcal{K}^{s}\ast\rho_{j}^{+}.

Clearly,
\bm{\rho}^{s}=(\rho_{1}^{s},\rho_{2}^{s})\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
is again a feasible pair of probability distributions, hence by minimality of
\bm{\rho}^{+}
, it holds

(38)

0\leq\liminf_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}^{s}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}^{+}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})}{s}\leq\limsup_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{s},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}-\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}}{2\tau s}+\liminf_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{s}.

It is well known that the heat flow is the metric gradient flow in the Wasserstein space of the (negative) entropy functional
\mathcal{H}(\rho)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho\log\rho\,\mathrm{d}x
, which is convex along generalized geodesics. By
[
2
, Theorem 4.0.4]
, it thus satisfies the evolutional variational inequality

\frac{1}{2}\limsup_{s\to 0^{+}}\,\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{j}^{s},\hat{\rho}_{j})^{2}-{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{j}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{j})^{2}}{s}\leq\mathcal{H}(\hat{\rho}_{j})-\mathcal{H}(\rho_{j}^{+}).

By combining the two components
j=1,2
and dividing by
\tau
, this yields

(39)

\limsup_{s\to 0^{+}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{s},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}-\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}}{2\tau s}\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}.

We now analyze the change in the energy term. We split it in the following way:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})-\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})]\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\rho_{1}^{s}K\ast\rho_{2}^{s}-\rho_{1}^{+}K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}]\,\mathrm{d}x.

For the interaction term, we use the representation
\rho_{j}^{s}=\mathcal{K}^{s}\ast\rho_{j}^{+}
together with the symmetry and semigroup property of the heat kernel
\mathcal{K}^{s}
and the properties of convolutions to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}^{s}K\ast\rho_{2}^{s}-\rho_{1}^{+}K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[(\mathcal{K}^{s}\ast\rho_{1}^{+})K\ast(\mathcal{K}^{s}\ast\rho_{2}^{+})-\rho_{1}^{+}K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}^{+}K\ast\mathcal{K}^{s}\ast\mathcal{K}^{s}\ast\rho_{2}^{+}-\rho_{1}^{+}K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}[K\ast\mathcal{K}^{2s}-K]\ast\rho_{2}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Hence by dominated convergence and the equation
\partial_{s}[K\ast\mathcal{K}^{s}]=\Delta[K\ast\mathcal{K}^{s}]
, it holds

(40)

\begin{split}\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{1}{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}^{s}K\ast\rho_{2}^{s}-\rho_{1}^{+}K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{K\ast\mathcal{K}^{2s}-K}{s}}}\right]\ast\rho_{2}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}\Delta K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2dC_{K}.\end{split}

The dominated convergence is justified, since from the assumption that
K
is convex with
\|\nabla^{2}K\|\leq C_{K}
and thus
0\leq\Delta K\leq dC_{K}
pointwise in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, it follows
0\leq\Delta[K\ast\mathcal{K}^{s}]\leq dC_{K}
pointwise in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
for every
s\geq 0
, which implies that the difference quotients are globally bounded by a constant.

For the
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
-term, we use that
\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}
is convex by Proposition
2.4
, and thus
s\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})\,\mathrm{d}x
is non-increasing along the heat flow
\bm{\rho}^{s}
: By Jensen and Fubini, we have

(41)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})\,\mathrm{d}x&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}(\mathcal{K}^{s}\ast\bm{\rho}^{+})\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{\rho}^{+}(y)\mathcal{K}^{s}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}(y))\mathcal{K}^{s}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\,\mathrm{d}y.\end{split}

Together with the identity
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}_{2\varepsilon}({\mathbf{r}})+\frac{1}{2}F_{1}(r_{1})+\frac{1}{2}F_{2}(r_{2})
, this implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})-\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{s})-F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{+})]\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[F_{2}(\rho_{2}^{s})-F_{2}(\rho_{2}^{+})]\,\mathrm{d}x.

We estimate the first integral (the second one is done in the same way) by replacing
\rho_{1}^{+}
with
