\rho_{1}^{s_{0}}
for some arbitrarily small
s_{0}>0
and then using a limiting argument. We use the fact that the
\rho_{j}^{\sigma}
solve the heat equation and Fubini to obtain for any
s_{0}>0
:

(42)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{s})-F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{s_{0}})]\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{s_{0}}^{s}F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{\sigma})\Delta\rho_{1}^{\sigma}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int_{s_{0}}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{\sigma})\Delta\rho_{1}^{\sigma}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma.

To justify the Fubini, it suffices to show that
F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{\sigma})
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and that
\Delta\rho_{1}^{\sigma}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
for
\sigma\in[s_{0},s]
. The first claim follows from global uniform boundedness of
\rho_{1}^{\sigma}=\mathcal{K}^{\sigma}\ast\rho_{1}^{+}
, which holds since
\mathcal{K}^{\sigma}(x)\leq\mathcal{K}^{s_{0}}(0)<+\infty
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and all
\sigma\in[s_{0},s]
. The second claim follows in a similar way from the fact that
\Delta\mathcal{K}^{\sigma}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
for
\sigma\in[s_{0},s]
.

By integrating (
42
) by parts, using that
\nabla[\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha}=\nabla\rho_{1}^{\sigma}
in
\{\rho_{1}^{\sigma}\leq\alpha\}
and
0
in
\{\rho_{1}^{\sigma}>\alpha\}
for every
\sigma\geq s_{0}
by regularity, and that
0\leq F_{1}^{\prime\prime}([\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha})\leq A
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we obtain

(43)

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{s})-F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{s_{0}})]\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int_{s_{0}}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{\sigma})\Delta\rho_{1}^{\sigma}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{s_{0}}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{\sigma})]\cdot\nabla\rho_{1}^{\sigma}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{s_{0}}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\rho_{1}^{\sigma})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\rho_{1}^{\sigma}}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma\\
&\leq-\frac{1}{2A}\int_{s_{0}}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla[\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha}}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma=-\frac{1}{2A}\int_{s_{0}}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}([\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma.\end{split}

We shall now pass to the limit
s_{0}\to 0
in this estimate. On the right-hand side, the integrand is non-negative, hence by monotone convergence, it holds

-\frac{1}{2A}\int_{s_{0}}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}([\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma\to-\frac{1}{2A}\int_{0}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}([\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma.

On the left-hand side, we observe that by convexity of
F_{1}
, the map
s\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{s})\,\mathrm{d}x
is non-increasing, which follows from Jensen’s inequality as in (
41
). In particular,

\limsup_{s_{0}\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{s_{0}})\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{+})\,\mathrm{d}x.

Passing to the limit
s_{0}\to 0
in (
43
) thus yields the estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{s})-F_{1}(\rho_{1}^{*})]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq-\frac{1}{2A}\int_{0}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}([\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma.

By adding the term for the other component and dividing by
-s<0
, this yields

(44)

\frac{1}{2As}\int_{0}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}([\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha})}}\right|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{2}^{\prime}([\rho_{2}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha})}}\right|^{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma\leq-\frac{1}{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})-\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})]\,\mathrm{d}x

By inserting (
39
) and (
40
) into (
38
), we can control the right hand side of (
44
) from above as
s\to 0
:

(45)

\begin{split}\limsup_{s\to 0^{+}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\frac{1}{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})-\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})]\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right]&=-\liminf_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})-\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})]\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+2dC_{K}\end{split}

By (
44
), this implies that
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{s}]_{\alpha})
is bounded in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}};{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, uniformly along a subsequence
s\to 0
. Hence by Alaoglu’s theorem, there exist
v_{j}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}};{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
such that
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{s}]_{\alpha})\rightharpoonup v_{j}
weakly in
L^{2}
globally up to subsequence. Additionally, since
F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{s}]_{\alpha})
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}
by
F_{j}^{\prime}(\alpha)
, we get that
F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{s}]_{\alpha})
is locally bounded in
H^{1}
, uniformly in
s
. Applying again Alaoglu’s theorem gives
u_{j}\in H^{1}_{loc}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
such that up to subsequence,
F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{s}]_{\alpha})\rightharpoonup u_{j}
weakly in
H^{1}_{loc}
as
s\to 0
. In particular,
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{s}]_{\alpha})\rightharpoonup\nabla u_{j}
weakly in
L^{2}_{loc}
, thus we can identify
v_{j}=\nabla u_{j}
. Also, we have
F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{s}]_{\alpha})\to u_{j}
strongly in
L^{2}
locally, which implies pointwise a.e. convergence to
u_{j}
along a further subsequence. It thus holds
u_{j}=F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{+}]_{\alpha})
, as
\rho_{j}^{s}\to\rho_{j}^{+}
a.e. pointwise. This identification shows that the whole sequence
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{s}]_{\alpha})
converges weakly in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. All together, we have shown
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{s}]_{\alpha})\rightharpoonup\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{+}]_{\alpha})
weakly in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
as
s\to 0
.

By lower semicontinuity of the
L^{2}
-norm with respect to weak convergence, this implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([\rho_{1}^{+}]_{\alpha}}}\right)}}\right|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{2}^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([\rho_{2}^{+}]_{\alpha}}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{2A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([\rho_{1}^{s}]_{\alpha}}}\right)}}\right|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{2}^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([\rho_{2}^{s}]_{\alpha}}}\right)}}\right|^{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{2As}\int_{0}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}([\rho_{1}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha})}}\right|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{2}^{\prime}([\rho_{2}^{\sigma}]_{\alpha})}}\right|^{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\sigma\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+2dC_{K},

where the last estimate follows from (
44
) and (
45
). This finishes the proof of (
37
).
∎

4.2.
Euler-Lagrange system for the minimizing movement step

As a next step, we derive an Euler-Lagrange system that minimizers
\bm{\rho}^{+}
satisfy. For simplicity, we prove it under additional assumptions on
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
, and later use approximation arguments to generalize our results.

Lemma 4.3
.

Assume in addition that both components of
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and that there exist positive radii
R_{j}>0
such that
\hat{\rho}_{j}>0
a.e. in
B_{R_{j}}
and
\hat{\rho}_{j}=0
a.e. outside of
B_{R_{j}}
. Fix any minimizer
\bm{\rho}^{+}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
over
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
obtained from Lemma
4.1
. Then there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}
and optimal pairs
(\varphi_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(\varphi_{2},\psi_{2})
of
c
-conjugate Kantorovich potentials for the (dual) transport problems of
\rho_{1}^{+}
to
\hat{\rho}_{1}
and of
\rho_{2}^{+}
to
\hat{\rho}_{2}
, respectively, such that
\bm{\rho}^{+}
satisfies the Euler-Lagrange system

(46)

\begin{split}F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})&=(C_{1}-\frac{\varphi_{1}}{\tau}-K\ast\rho_{2}^{+})_{+}\\
F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})&=(C_{2}-\frac{\varphi_{2}}{\tau}-K\ast\rho_{1}^{+})_{+}\,.\end{split}

The optimal maps
T_{1},T_{2}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, transporting
\rho_{1}^{+}
to
\hat{\rho}_{1}
and
\rho_{2}^{+}
to
\hat{\rho}_{2}
, respectively, satisfy

(47)

\begin{split}\frac{T_{1}(x)-x}{\tau}&=\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}}}\right]\qquad\mathcal{L}^{d}\text{-a.e. in }\{\rho_{1}^{+}>0\}\\
\frac{T_{2}(x)-x}{\tau}&=\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{1}^{+}}}\right]\qquad\mathcal{L}^{d}\text{-a.e. in }\{\rho_{2}^{+}>0\}.\end{split}

Proof.

Take
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
and
\bm{\rho}^{+}
as above. We only need to show the first equation in (
46
) and (
47
), since the other one is analogous in both cases.

Fix any
\eta_{0}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
which is compactly supported, bounded, satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x=0
and
\rho_{1}^{s}:=\rho_{1}^{+}+s\eta_{0}\geq 0
for every sufficiently small
s>0
, and there exists a constant
H>0
such that
\eta_{0}
vanishes almost everywhere on
\{\rho_{1}^{+}>H\}
, similarly as in the proof of Lemma
3.5
. Then for all sufficiently small
s>0
, it holds
\rho_{1}^{s}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and thus by minimality of
\rho_{1}^{+}
for the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}((\cdot,\rho_{2}^{+})|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
over
{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, we have with
\bm{\rho}^{s}:=(\rho_{1}^{s},\rho_{2}^{+})
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{s\to 0^{+}}\,\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}^{s}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}^{+}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})}{s}=\frac{1}{2\tau}\liminf_{s\to 0^{+}}\,\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{s},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}-{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}}{s}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}s}}}\right|_{s=0^{+}}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})

(48)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\tau}\liminf_{s\to 0^{+}}\,\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{s},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}-{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}}{s}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}}}\right]\,\eta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x.

The last equality follows as in the proof of Lemma
3.5
from the dominated convergence theorem. Our goal is to construct a pair
(\varphi_{1}^{0},\psi_{1}^{0})
of optimal Kantorovich potentials for the transport problem from
\rho_{1}^{+}
to
\hat{\rho}_{1}
, such that

(49)

\frac{1}{2}\liminf_{s\to 0^{+}}\,\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{s},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}-{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}}{s}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{1}^{0}\,\eta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Denote by
(\varphi_{1}^{s},\psi_{1}^{s})
pairs of optimal Kantorovich potentials for the transport from
\rho_{1}^{s}
to
\hat{\rho}_{1}
. We can fix some point
x_{0}\in\mathrm{supp}\,\rho_{1}^{+}
and without loss of generality assume
\varphi_{1}^{s}(x_{0})=0
for every
s
. Since
\{\rho_{1}^{+}>0\}\subset\{\rho_{1}^{s}>0\}
for every
s\in[0,\bar{s}]
with some
\bar{s}>0
, the pair
(\varphi_{1}^{s},\psi_{1}^{s})
is admissible for the (dual) transport problem from
\rho_{1}^{+}
to
\hat{\rho}_{1}
. Hence for every
s\in[0,\bar{s}]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{s},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}-{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}}}\right]\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\varphi_{1}^{s}\,\rho_{1}^{s}+\psi_{1}^{s}\,\hat{\rho}_{1}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\varphi_{1}^{s}\,\rho_{1}^{+}+\psi_{1}^{s}\,\hat{\rho}_{1}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x=s\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{1}^{s}\,\eta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x.

The proof of (
49
) thus reduces to the construction of an optimal potential
\varphi_{1}^{0}
for the transport problem from
\rho_{1}^{+}
to
\hat{\rho}_{1}
such that

(50)

\liminf_{s\to 0^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{1}^{s}\,\eta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{1}^{0}\,\eta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Denote
\Phi_{1}^{s}:=\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\varphi_{1}^{s}
. Since
(\varphi_{1}^{s},\psi_{1}^{s})
is a pair of optimal
c
-conjugate potentials,
\Phi_{1}^{s}
is a convex function and satisfies
|\nabla\Phi_{1}^{s}(x)|=|T^{s}(x)|
for almost every
x\in\{\rho_{1}^{s}>0\}
, where
T^{s}\#\rho_{1}^{s}=\hat{\rho}_{1}
is the optimal transport map from
\rho_{1}^{s}
to
\hat{\rho}_{1}
. Since by assumption,
\hat{\rho}_{1}
vanishes a.e. outside of
B_{R_{1}}
, this implies
|\nabla\Phi_{1}^{s}|\leq R_{1}
a.e. on
\{\rho_{1}^{s}>0\}
. Since
\Phi_{1}^{s}
is convex on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, this implies existence of a convex function
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}:{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}=\Phi_{1}^{s}
in
\{\rho_{1}^{s}>0\}
and
|\nabla\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}|\leq R_{1}
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. Hence the family of potentials
\{\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\}_{s\in[0,\bar{s}]}
is equi-Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and because
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}(x_{0})=\Phi_{1}^{s}(x_{0})=0
for all
s
, it is bounded pointwise. The Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem thus yields existence of a locally uniformly converging subsequence
(\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s_{k}})_{k}\to\Phi_{1}^{0}
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.

Clearly,
\varphi_{1}^{0}:=\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\Phi_{1}^{0}
satisfies (
50
). It remains to show that
\varphi_{1}^{0}
, together with its
c
-conjugate
\psi_{1}^{0}
, forms an optimal pair for the transport from
\rho_{1}^{+}
to
\hat{\rho}_{1}
. Since
\Phi_{1}^{0}
is convex, it is sufficient to show
\nabla\Phi_{1}^{0}\#\rho_{1}^{+}=\hat{\rho}_{1}
. This however follows from the fact that the
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}
are convex and
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s_{k}}\to\Phi_{1}^{0}
locally uniformly, which implies
\nabla\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s_{k}}\to\nabla\Phi_{1}^{0}
almost everywhere: For any
\zeta\in C^{0}_{c}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, it holds by dominated convergence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta\,\nabla\Phi_{1}^{0}\#\rho_{1}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta(\nabla\Phi_{1}^{0}(x))\,\rho_{1}^{+}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta(\nabla\tilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s_{k}}(x))\,\rho_{1}^{+}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta\,\hat{\rho}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x.

All together, we have proven the inequality (
49
) for the optimal potential
\varphi_{1}^{0}
.

We can insert this result into (
48
) to obtain

0\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}+\frac{\varphi_{1}^{0}}{\tau}}}\right]\,\eta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x.

In order to obtain the Euler-Lagrange system (
46
), we have to show that for some optimal Kantorovich potential
\varphi_{1}
, this inequality holds true for all
\eta
which satisfy our assumptions at once, and not just for the specific
\eta_{0}
used above to construct the potentials
\varphi_{1}^{0}
and
\psi_{1}^{0}
. We define, using the notations
\Phi_{1}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\varphi_{1}^{0}
and
\Psi_{1}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\psi_{1}^{0}
, the functions

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Psi_{1}^{0}&\text{ in }B_{R_{1}}\\
+\infty&\text{ else}\end{array}}}\right.,\quad\Phi_{1}:=(\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{0})^{*},

where
(\cdot)^{*}
denotes the convex conjugate. Since
\hat{\rho}_{1}
is supported in
B_{R_{1}}
, it follows that
\Psi_{1}
is convex with
\nabla\Psi_{1}\#\hat{\rho}_{1}=\nabla\Psi_{1}^{0}\#\hat{\rho}_{1}=\rho_{1}^{+}
, implying that the pair of
c
-conjugate potentials
(\varphi_{1},\psi_{1}):=(\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\Phi_{1},\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\Psi_{1})
is optimal. We claim that for all
\eta
that have the properties of
\eta_{0}
specified above, it holds

(51)

0\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}+\frac{\varphi_{1}}{\tau}}}\right]\,\eta\,\mathrm{d}x.

To prove this, take any such
\eta
. By the same construction as for
\eta_{0}
, there exists a pair of optimal potentials
(\tilde{\varphi}_{1},\tilde{\psi}_{1})
, such that (
51
) holds with
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}
instead of
\varphi_{1}
. Now, observe that
\tilde{\Psi}_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\tilde{\psi}_{1}
is a convex function whose gradient agrees with the gradient of
\Psi_{1}^{0}
a.e. on the connected set
\{\hat{\rho}_{1}>0\}=B_{R_{1}}
, since by absolute continuity of
\hat{\rho}_{1}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure, the optimal transport map from
\hat{\rho}_{1}
to
\rho_{1}^{+}
is unique and given by the gradient of any one of the convex potentials
\Psi_{1}^{0}
and
\tilde{\Psi}_{1}
. Hence there exists a global constant
C
such that
\Psi_{1}^{0}=\tilde{\Psi}_{1}+C
in
B_{R_{1}}
. By definition of
\Psi_{1}
, this yields
\Psi_{1}\geq\tilde{\Psi}_{1}+C
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with equality in
B_{R_{1}}
. By the properties of the convex conjugate, this implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\varphi_{1}=\Phi_{1}=\Psi_{1}^{*}\leq\tilde{\Psi}_{1}^{*}-C=\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\tilde{\varphi}_{1}-C,

or equivalently,
\varphi_{1}\geq\tilde{\varphi}_{1}+C
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, with equality almost everywhere on the image of the map
\nabla\Psi_{1}
over
B_{R_{1}}
, which is
\{\rho_{1}^{+}>0\}
since
\nabla\Psi_{1}
pushes
\hat{\rho}_{1}
to
\rho_{1}^{+}
. Using that
\eta\geq 0
in
\{\rho_{1}^{+}=0\}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta=0
, this gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{1}\eta\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\{\rho_{1}^{+}>0\}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{\varphi}_{1}+C}}\right)\,\eta\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\{\rho_{1}^{+}=0\}}\varphi_{1}\eta\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{\varphi}_{1}+C}}\right)\,\eta\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tilde{\varphi}_{1}\eta\,\mathrm{d}x,

proving (
51
), since we have already shown that it holds with
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}
instead of
\varphi_{1}
.

By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
3.6
, (
51
) yields the existence of some constant
C_{1}
, such that everywhere in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, it holds

F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}+\frac{\varphi_{1}}{\tau}\geq C_{1},

with equality almost everywhere on
\{\rho_{1}^{+}>0\}
. The form (
46
) then follows in the same way as Lemma
3.7
from the properties of
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
.
Hence the optimal potential
\varphi_{1}
satisfies the first equation in (
46
). The equation for the other component is derived analogously.

Since the left-hand side of (
46
) is strictly positive on
\{\rho_{j}^{+}>0\}
, so is the right-hand side. The Kantorovich potentials are differentiable almost everywhere in
\{\rho_{j}^{+}>0\}
with
-\nabla\varphi_{j}=T_{j}(x)-x
, hence the gradient of the right-hand side exists in
\{\rho_{j}^{+}>0\}
, and the equations (
47
) follow from (
46
) by taking the gradient on both sides.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
4.3
, we prove that under the same assumptions, the transition from
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
to
\bm{\rho}^{+}
satisfies a time-discrete version of the weak formulation of the evolution equations (
1
).

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
4.3
. Then for all
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, there hold the estimates

(52)

\begin{split}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\rho_{1}^{+}-\hat{\rho}_{1}}{\tau}\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right|&\leq\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}}{2\tau}\,\|\zeta\|_{C^{2}}\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\rho_{2}^{+}-\hat{\rho}_{2}}{\tau}\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{2}^{+}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{1}^{+}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right|&\leq\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{2}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{2})^{2}}{2\tau}\,\|\zeta\|_{C^{2}}.\end{split}

Denoting by
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
the respective left-hand sides of these estimates, it holds

(53)

\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}}\right)\,\|\zeta\|_{C^{2}}.

Proof.

Let
T_{1}\#\rho_{1}^{+}=\hat{\rho}_{1}
be the optimal transport map, and let
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
be arbitrary. We obtain for the difference quotient on the left-hand side

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\rho_{1}^{+}-\hat{\rho}_{1}}{\tau}\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}T_{1}\#\rho_{1}^{+}\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}(x)\,\frac{\zeta(x)-\zeta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{1}(x)}}\right)}{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}(x)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\nabla\zeta(x)\cdot\frac{T_{1}(x)-x}{\tau}+\frac{(T_{1}(x)-x)\cdot\nabla^{2}\zeta(\xi_{x})(T_{1}(x)-x)}{2\tau}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x

for some
\xi_{x}\in[x,T_{1}(x)]
. Applying (
47
) and
{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}(x)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|T_{1}(x)-x}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
thus yields

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\rho_{1}^{+}-\hat{\rho}_{1}}{\tau}\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\rho_{1}^{+}-\hat{\rho}_{1}}{\tau}\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}(x)\,\nabla\zeta(x)\cdot\frac{T_{1}(x)-x}{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}(x)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{(T_{1}(x)-x)\cdot\nabla^{2}\zeta(\xi_{x})(T_{1}(x)-x)}{2\tau}}}\right|\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}}{2\tau}\,\|\zeta\|_{C^{2}}.

This proves the first inequality in (
52
), the second one follows analogously. By adding the two inequalities and applying (
36
), we obtain (
53
).
∎

4.3.
Regularity estimates for the minimizers

In order to prove convergence of the minimizing movement scheme to a weak solution of (
1
), we need to prove the estimates from Corollary
4.4
for more general
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
, which will be done using an approximating argument. For the convergence proof, we require further regularity results for the minimizers
\bm{\rho}^{+}
, in particular, a global
H^{1}
-bound for
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
. This will follow from the next results, together with the bound on
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
from Lemma
4.2
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
4.3
. Then it holds

(54)

\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla[F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 4\,\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+\frac{8C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\,.

Proof.

Since
K\ast\rho_{j}^{+}
is differentiable everywhere, the Euler-Lagrange equations in the form (
47
) imply that the gradients on the left-hand side are well-defined a.e. in
\{\rho_{j}^{+}>0\}
. For
j=1
(the other term is analogous), it holds

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{1}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{1}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla[K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{1}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\tau^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|T_{1}(x)-x|^{2}\,\rho_{1}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{4C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{2}{\tau^{2}}\,{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1}^{+},\hat{\rho}_{1})^{2}+\frac{4C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}),

where the second to last inequality uses (
47
) for the first integral, and the fact that
K
, and thus
K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}
, is non-negative and
\lambda
-convex with its second derivative bounded by
C_{K}
for the second integral. Adding the analogous term for
j=2
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla[F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{2}{\tau^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}+\frac{8C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}),

which together with (
36
) yields the claimed estimate (
54
).
∎

In order to obtain a global
H^{1}
-estimate for the functions
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
, we first need to prove existence of their gradients. This follows directly from the following lemma, which is a consequence of the Euler-Lagrange system (
46
).

Lemma 4.6
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
4.3
, the
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
are locally Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.

Proof.

This follows from (
46
) in the same way as in Lemma
3.11
, since the right-hand side of (
46
) is locally Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. Note that the Kantorovich potentials
\varphi_{j}
are of the form
\frac{1}{2}|\cdot|^{2}-\Phi_{j}
, where
\Phi_{j}
are convex functions which are finite in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, since the left-hand side of (
46
) is finite everywhere.
∎

With this regularity result, we can prove the following estimate for
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
:

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
4.3
, and assume that
\varepsilon
is such that
3\kappa^{2}\varepsilon^{2}<1
, where
\kappa^{2}:=\max\{\kappa_{1,1},\kappa_{1,2}\}^{2}+\max\{\kappa_{2,1},\kappa_{2,2}\}^{2}
with the constants
\kappa_{j,i}
from Hypothesis
2.2
. Then there exists a constant
B
, independent of
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
and
\bm{\rho}^{+}
, such that

\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq B\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}}\right).

Proof.

The gradient of
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
exists almost everywhere by local Lipschitz-continuity, and the same is true for
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})
, see the proof of Lemma
4.5
. Thus, the gradient of
\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})
is also well-defined in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. The claim will now follow from Lemma
4.5
and Hypothesis
2.2
: With
\bm{u}^{+}:=(F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2}^{+}))
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})=\nabla\theta_{j}(\bm{u}^{+})=\theta_{j,1}(\bm{u}^{+})\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\theta_{j,2}(\bm{u}^{+})\nabla F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2}^{+}).

By splitting the integral, we thus obtain for
j=1,2
the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})}}\right]-\varepsilon\sum_{i=1,2}\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u}^{+})\nabla F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 3\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla[F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x+3\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{i=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\theta^{2}_{j,i}(\bm{u}^{+})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Summing over
j=1,2
and applying (
54
) for the first term and (
15
) for the other two yields

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 12\,\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+\frac{24C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})

\displaystyle+3\kappa^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{i=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{i}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Subtracting the last term and using
3\kappa^{2}\varepsilon^{2}<1
yields the claim with
B=\frac{12}{1-3\kappa^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}
.
∎

What makes Lemma
4.7
important is that it controls
|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})|^{2}
on the set where
\rho_{j}^{+}
is large. Together with Lemma
4.2
, which controls the same gradient when
\rho_{j}^{+}
is small, this implies an explicit global
L^{2}
-bound for
\nabla F_{j}(\rho_{j}^{+})
:

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
4.3
. Let
\alpha>0
be arbitrary, and denote by
A
and
B
the constants from Lemmas
4.2
and
4.7
, respectively. Then it holds

(55)

\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})+\frac{\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+2dC_{K}}}\right),

with the constant
C:=\max\{\frac{B}{\alpha},2A\}
.

Proof.

For
j=1,2
, we split the integral into one part where
\rho_{j}^{+}
is large and one where it is small. By local Lipschitz-continuity, it holds
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})=\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{+}]_{\alpha})
on
\{\rho_{j}^{+}<\alpha\}
, hence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\{\rho_{j}^{+}\geq\alpha\}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\{\rho_{j}^{+}<\alpha\}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}([\rho_{j}^{+}]_{\alpha})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

The claim now follows by summing over
j=1,2
and applying Lemmas
4.7
and
4.2
.
∎

4.4.
Generalization of the time-discrete error estimates

With the help of the estimate from Corollary
4.8
, we can now prove the time-discrete error estimate (
53
) for much more general
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
. Concretely, we prove the following theorem:

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
be such that
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})<+\infty
. Then there exists a minimizer
\bm{\rho}^{+}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
of the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
, such that for all
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
:

\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}}\right)\,\|\zeta\|_{C^{2}}\,,

where
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
are the integral errors defined as in Corollary
4.4
.

Proof.

In Corollary
4.4
, the claim has already been shown under the more restrictive assumptions that both components of
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
are strictly positive almost everywhere inside balls
B_{R_{j}}
and vanish almost everywhere else. We prove the more general case by approximating
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
by a sequence of pairs that satisfy these stronger assumptions.

Let
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
be as above. Without loss of generality, we can assume
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. We start by showing that the assumption
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})<+\infty
implies that
\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})<+\infty
as well. To see this, observe that by comparing the asymptotic growths of the derivatives, it holds
r\log r\leq cF_{j}(r)
for every
r\geq 0
with some constant
c
. It thus holds for every
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}\log\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\log\rho_{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}(\rho_{1})+F_{2}(\rho_{2})}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2c\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}),

proving that
\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})
is finite.

For
j=1,2
, we now take a sequence of positive radii
0<R_{j,1}<R_{j,2}<\dots
such that
R_{j,n}\nearrow

R_{j,\infty}:=\sup\,\{R>0:\mathrm{supp}\,\hat{\rho}_{j}\nsubseteq B_{R}\}\in(0,+\infty]
. Further, we fix constants
H_{j},C_{j}>0
such that for every
n
, it holds

|B_{R_{j,n}}^{\prime}|\geq C_{j}\,,\quad\text{where }B_{R_{j,n}}^{\prime}:=\{\hat{\rho}_{j}\leq H_{j}\}\cap B_{R_{j,n}}.

Define for every
n
the probability densities

\hat{\rho}_{j,n}:=\mathds{1}_{B_{R_{j,n}}}\,\hat{\rho}_{j}+\frac{m_{j,n}}{|B_{R_{j,n}}^{\prime}|}\mathds{1}_{B_{R_{j,n}}^{\prime}}\,,\quad\text{with }m_{j,n}:=1-\int_{B_{R_{j,n}}}\hat{\rho}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\in(0,1].

For the densities
\hat{\rho}_{j,n}
, we have
\{\hat{\rho}_{j,n}>0\}=B_{R_{j,n}}
. Hence, the pairs
\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}:=(\hat{\rho}_{1,n},\hat{\rho}_{2,n})
satisfy the assumptions of Corollary
4.4
, and thus for every minimizer
\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\rho}_{n})
, the estimate (
53
). The same estimate for
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
, which is the claim, thus follows if we prove for
n\to\infty
the convergences

(56)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\rho_{1,n}^{+}-\hat{\rho}_{1,n}}{\tau}\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\rightarrow\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\rho_{1}^{+}-\hat{\rho}_{1}}{\tau}\,\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x,

(57)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1,n}^{+}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1,n}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2,n}^{+}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\rightarrow\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\,,\end{split}

(58)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}_{n})}}\right)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})

for some minimizer
\bm{\rho}^{+}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
, up to subsequence. The convergence of the other component’s terms will follow analogously.

We start by observing that by construction,
\hat{\rho}_{j,n}\to\hat{\rho}_{j}
in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and with respect to the
{\mathbf{W}}
-metric. Additionally, it holds that
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})\to\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})
and
\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})\to\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})
, which can be shown from the construction by dominated convergence. In particular, since
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})
and
\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})
are finite,
(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}))_{n}
and
(\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}))_{n}
are bounded sequences. Since it holds
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})
, the sequence
(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}_{n}))_{n}
is thus bounded as well. By Lemma
4.1
, it holds
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}^{+}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}^{+}]={\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{1}^{n}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{2}^{n}]\to{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{1}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\hat{\rho}_{2}]=0
. Lemma
3.1
thus yields
\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}\rightharpoonup\bm{\rho}^{+}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
weakly in
L^{1}
up to subsequence. We check that
\bm{\rho}^{+}
is a global minimizer of the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
. Indeed, it holds

(59)

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})

by weak
L^{1}
-convergence of the
\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}
and Lemma
3.2
. By lower semicontinuity of the distance with respect to weak
L^{1}
-convergence, the triangle inequality and
\mathrm{d}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n},\hat{\bm{\rho}})\to 0
, we also get

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})^{2}.

Together with the minimality of
\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}
for
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})
, this implies for every
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}^{+}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})=\frac{1}{2\tau}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2\tau}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+},\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2\tau}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho},\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})}}\right)=\frac{1}{2\tau}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho},\hat{\bm{\rho}})^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}|\hat{\bm{\rho}})\,,

proving that
\bm{\rho}^{+}
is a minimizer of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
.

The convergence (
56
) is clear by narrow convergence of
\hat{\rho}_{1,n}\to\hat{\rho}_{1}
and
\rho_{1,n}^{+}\to\rho_{1}^{+}
. Since (
59
) and
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})\to\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})
imply the inequality (
58
), it remains to prove (
57
). We show this convergence by proving that in
L^{2}(S)
with
S:=\mathrm{supp}\,\zeta\subset\subset{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, it holds for
j=1,2
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})\rightharpoonup\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+}),\quad\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})&\rightharpoonup\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+}),\quad\nabla K\ast\rho_{j,n}^{+}\rightharpoonup\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{+}\text{ weakly,}\\
\rho_{j,n}^{+}&\to\rho_{j}^{+}\text{ strongly.}\end{split}

We start by showing that
(F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+}))_{n}
is a bounded sequence in
H^{1}(S)
. In order to apply Corollary
4.8
, note that the sequences
(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}))_{n}
,
(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}_{n}))_{n}
and
(\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}))_{n}
are bounded from above, and that
(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}_{n}))_{n}
and
(\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{+}_{n}))_{n}
are bounded from below, which follows from non-negativity of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
and the general inequality

(60)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho\log\rho\,\mathrm{d}x\geq 1-{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho]-\pi^{d/2}\qquad\text{ for all }\rho\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}}),

and from the boundedness of the second moments of
\rho_{j,n}^{+}
. Hence, the right hand sides of (
55
) are bounded from above by a constant for all
n
, which implies uniform boundedness of
\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho^{+}_{1,n})
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. In order to also get uniform boundedness of
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})
in
L^{2}(S)
, note that
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})
is uniformly bounded in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
by inequality (
24
), thus uniform boundedness in
L^{2}(S)
follows from the Poincaré inequality in the smooth and bounded set
S
.
Hence, the sequence
(F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+}))_{n}
is bounded in
H^{1}(S)
. Alaoglu’s and Rellich’s theorems thus imply
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})\to u_{j}^{+}
strongly in
L^{2}(S)
, weakly in
H^{1}(S)
, for some
u_{j}^{+}\in H^{1}(S)
. The identification
u_{j}^{+}=F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
now follows from the narrow convergence
\rho_{j,n}^{+}\to\rho_{j}^{+}
, since
\rho_{j,n}^{+}\to(F_{j}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{j}^{+})
pointwise almost everywhere along a subsequence. This finishes the proof of the weak
L^{2}(S)
-convergence
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})\rightharpoonup\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
.

In order to obtain weak
L^{2}(S)
-convergence
\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})\rightharpoonup\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})
, we write

\displaystyle\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})=\nabla\theta_{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1,n}^{+}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2,n}^{+})}}\right)=\sum_{i=1,2}\theta_{j,i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1,n}^{+}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2,n}^{+})}}\right)\nabla F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i,n}^{+}).

Denoting
\bm{u}_{n}^{+}:=(u_{1,n}^{+},u_{2,n}^{+}):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1,n}^{+}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2,n}^{+})}}\right)
, it thus follows from assumption (
15
):

\displaystyle\|\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})\|_{L^{2}(S)}\leq\sum_{i=1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{S}\theta^{2}_{j,i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{u}_{n}^{+}}}\right)|\nabla u_{i,n}^{+}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sum_{i=1,2}\kappa_{j,i}\,\|u_{i,n}^{+}\|_{L^{2}(S)},

which is uniformly bounded for all
n
, since
u_{i,n}^{+}=F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i,n}^{+})
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(S)
. Hence up to subsequence,
\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})\rightharpoonup v
for some
v\in L^{2}(S;{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. In order to identify the limit
v=\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})
, notice that for every
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(S;{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{S}\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})\cdot\psi\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{S}\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})\ \mathrm{div}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\to-\int_{S}\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})\ \mathrm{div}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{S}\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})\cdot\psi\,\mathrm{d}x,

where we have used that
\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})\to\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})
strongly in
L^{2}(S)
, which follows from
\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})=\theta_{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1,n}^{+}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2,n}^{+})}}\right)
and the
L^{2}(S)
-convergence
F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i,n}^{+})\to F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i}^{+})
, since
\theta_{j}
is globally
(\kappa_{j,1}+\kappa_{j,2})
-Lipschitz by (
15
). This implies
v=\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})
.

To prove
\nabla K\ast\rho_{j,n}^{+}\rightharpoonup\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{+}
weakly in
L^{2}(S)
, fix any
\varphi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}};{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
with
\varphi\equiv 0
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\setminus S
. It now holds

\displaystyle\int_{S}\varphi\cdot\nabla K\ast\rho_{j,n}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\nabla K\ast\varphi)\,\rho_{j,n}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x\to-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\nabla K\ast\varphi)\,\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{S}\varphi\cdot\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x,

since
\rho_{j,n}^{+}\to\rho_{j}^{+}
narrowly with uniformly bounded second moments, and since
\nabla K\ast\varphi
is continuous and grows at most linearly by the growth assumption on
K
.

It remains to prove
\rho_{j,n}^{+}\to\rho_{j}^{+}
strongly in
L^{2}(S)
, which we derive from the strong
L^{2}(S)
-convergence of
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})\to F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
. Since for non-negative functions
u_{n}
and
u
, the convergence
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{2}
is equivalent to the convergence
u_{n}^{2}\to u^{2}
in
L^{1}
, it is sufficient to show that every subsequence of
((\rho_{j,n}^{+})^{2})_{n}
contains a further subsequence that converges to
(\rho_{j}^{+})^{2}
strongly in
L^{1}(S)
. Since strong
L^{1}(S)
-convergence follows from equi-integrability together with pointwise convergence almost everywhere, and since
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})\to F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
strongly in
L^{2}(S)
, we only need to prove equi-integrability of the sequence
((\rho_{j,n}^{+})^{2})_{n}
in
S
. By the
L^{1}(S)
-convergence
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})^{2}\to F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})^{2}
, the sequence
(F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})^{2})_{n}
is equi-integrable in
S
. It thus follows that

\displaystyle\rho_{j,n}^{2}=g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})^{2}}}\right)\quad\text{ with }g(r):=(F_{j}^{\prime})^{-1}(\sqrt{r})^{2}

is also equi-integrable in
S
, since the non-decreasing function
g
grows at most linearly for large
r
by assumption (
12
) on
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}
. This finishes the proof of
\rho_{j,n}^{+}\to\rho_{j}^{+}
strongly in
L^{2}(S)
, thus concluding the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

From the approximation argument in the proof above, we also obtain the following generalization of Corollary
4.8
: If
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
is such that
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})<+\infty
, it holds

(61)

\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})+\frac{\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}{\tau}+2dC_{K}}}\right),

where the only difference to (
55
) is the appearance of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})
instead of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})
in the second term. To see this, notice that (
55
) holds for every
\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}
and
\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}
from the proof of Theorem
4.9
. On the left-hand side, we can pass to the limit
n\to\infty
since
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})\rightharpoonup\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})
weakly in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
and the
L^{2}
-norm is weakly lower semicontinuous. The weak
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
-convergence follows from the uniform boundedness of
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})
that is obtained from (
55
), Alaoglu’s theorem and the weak
L^{2}_{loc}
-convergence proven above. On the right-hand side, we use that
\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})\to\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})
,
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\leq\liminf_{n}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})
,
\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\leq\liminf_{n}\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})
and
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})=\lim_{n}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})\geq\limsup_{n}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})
, to pass to the limit and obtain (
61
).

4.5.
Interpolation and convergence to weak solution

We fix any initial data
\bm{\rho}^{0}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})
is finite. For every
\tau>0
, we iteratively define a sequence of feasible pairs
(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})_{n}=(\hat{\rho}_{1,n},\hat{\rho}_{2,n})_{n}\subset{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
in the following way:

(62)

\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{0}:=\bm{\rho}^{0},\qquad\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}:=\bm{\rho}_{n-1}^{+}\in\argmin_{\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\bm{\rho}|\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n-1})\ \text{ for all }n\geq 1

where the minimizer
\bm{\rho}_{n-1}^{+}
is chosen to be the one constructed in Theorem
4.9
. Notice that the assumption
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n-1})<+\infty
for the theorem is fulfilled in every iteration, as
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n-1})
by Lemma
4.1
.
We now define the piecewise constant interpolating curve
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}=(\rho_{1}^{\tau},\rho_{2}^{\tau}):[0,+\infty)\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
as

\displaystyle\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t):=\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n+1}=\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}\quad\text{ for all }t\text{ such that }n\tau<t\leq(n+1)\tau.

The goal is to prove that for some subsequence
\tau\downarrow 0
, the curves
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}
converge in some topology to a continuous limit curve
\bm{\rho}:[0,+\infty)\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, which is a weak solution to the evolution equations (
1
). We first prove that the piecewise constant interpolations
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}
satisfy a weak formulation of the system up to a small error, which is a direct consequence of Theorem
4.9
.

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
be arbitrary. Define for
\tau>0
the time-discrete approximation
\zeta^{\tau}
on
[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
as
\zeta^{\tau}(t,x):=\zeta_{n}(x):=\zeta(n\tau,x)
for
n\tau\leq t<(n+1)\tau
. Then it holds

(63)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}^{\tau}\,\partial_{t}\zeta-\rho_{1}^{\tau}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{\tau})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{\tau},\rho_{2}^{\tau})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{\tau}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta^{\tau}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{\rho}_{1}\zeta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right|\leq C\tau,

and analogously for the other component, where
C=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})\,\|\zeta\|_{C^{2}}
is independent of
\tau
.

Proof.

Since
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}
and
\zeta^{\tau}
do not depend on
t
in every interval
(n\tau,(n+1)\tau)
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{\tau}\,\partial_{t}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{n\tau}^{(n+1)\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1,n}^{+}\,\partial_{t}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1,n}^{+}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\zeta_{n+1}-\zeta_{n}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\tau\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\hat{\rho}_{1,n}-\rho_{1,n}^{+}}{\tau}\,\zeta_{n}\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{\rho}_{1}\zeta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{\tau}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{\tau})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{\tau},\rho_{2}^{\tau})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{\tau}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta^{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\tau\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1,n}^{+}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1,n}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1,n}^{+},\rho_{2,n}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{2,n}^{+}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta_{n}\,\mathrm{d}x.

By applying Theorem
4.9
, we thus obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}^{\tau}\,\partial_{t}\zeta-\rho_{1}^{\tau}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{\tau})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{\tau},\rho_{2}^{\tau})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{\tau}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta^{\tau}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{\rho}_{1}\zeta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\tau\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}_{n})}}\right)\,\|\zeta_{n}\|_{C^{2}}\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})\,\|\zeta\|_{C^{2}}\tau,

where we have used
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})\geq 0
,
\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}=\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n+1}
and
\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{0}=\bm{\rho}^{0}
. The proof for the other component is analogous.
∎

In order to establish convergence of
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}
to some limiting curve
\bm{\rho}
, we first prove the following a priori estimate on the jump sizes of the piecewise constant curves
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}
:

Lemma 4.12
.

For every
\tau>0
and every
s,t\geq 0
, it holds

(64)

\mathrm{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(s),\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)^{2}\leq 2\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|s-t|+\tau}}\right)

Proof.

Fix
\tau>0
. By Lemma
4.1
, it holds
\mathrm{d}(\hat{\bm{\rho}},\bm{\rho}^{+})^{2}\leq 2\tau(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}))
for all
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
. Thus for all integers
m\geq n\geq 0
and every
s,t\geq 0
such that
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(s)=\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}
and
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)=\bm{\rho}_{m}^{+}
, the triangle inequality for the metric
\mathrm{d}
and the identity
\bm{\rho}_{k}^{+}=\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{k+1}
yield

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(s),\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)^{2}=\mathrm{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+},\bm{\rho}_{m}^{+}}}\right)^{2}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{k=n}^{m-1}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}_{k}^{+},\bm{\rho}_{k+1}^{+})}}\right)^{2}\leq(m-n)\sum_{k=n}^{m-1}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}_{k}^{+},\bm{\rho}_{k+1}^{+})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\tau(m-n)\sum_{k=n}^{m-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{k}^{+})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{k+1}^{+})}}\right)=2\tau(m-n)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{m}^{+})}}\right)\leq 2\tau(m-n)\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0}).

By construction of
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}
, we have
s\leq(n+1)\tau
and
t>m\tau
, which imply
(m-n)\tau\leq t-s+\tau
. Inserting into the above estimate yields (
64
). The case
n>m
is analogous.
∎

With the help of Lemma
4.12
, we prove the following convergence result for
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}
:

Lemma 4.13
.

There exists a subsequence
\tau\downarrow 0
and a curve
\bm{\rho}^{*}:[0,+\infty)\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, which is Hölder-
\frac{1}{2}
-continuous with respect to the metric
\mathrm{d}
, such that
\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\rightharpoonup\rho_{j}^{*}(t)
weakly in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
at every
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

For every
t\geq 0
and
\tau>0
, it holds
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
and

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t))\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})<+\infty
.
Lemma
3.1
thus implies weak
L^{1}
-compactness of the sequence at every
t\geq 0
. By a diagonal argument, this implies existence of a single subsequence
\tau\downarrow 0
, along which
\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)
is weakly
L^{1}
-convergent to some
\rho_{j}^{*}(t)
at every
t\in{\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,+\infty)
for both
j
. By lower semicontinuity of the distance
\mathrm{d}
with respect to weak
L^{1}
-convergence and Lemma
4.12
, it holds for every
s,t\in{\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,+\infty)
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{*}(s),\bm{\rho}^{*}(t))^{2}\leq\liminf_{\tau}\mathrm{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(s),\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)^{2}\leq\liminf_{\tau}2\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|s-t|+\tau}}\right)=2\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})|s-t|.

Hence, the function
\bm{\rho}^{*}:{\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,+\infty)\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
is Hölder-
\frac{1}{2}
-continuous. Since
({\mathfrak{X}}_{0},\mathrm{d})
is a complete metric space, there exists a unique (Hölder-
\frac{1}{2}
-)continuous extension
\bm{\rho}^{*}:[0,+\infty)\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. The claim that
\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\rightharpoonup\rho_{j}^{*}(t)
weakly at every
t\geq 0
now follows directly from weak compactness, since
\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\rightharpoonup\rho_{j}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
implies by the same arguments for every
s\in{\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,+\infty)
the estimate

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho},\bm{\rho}^{*}(s))^{2}\leq 2\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})|s-t|

and thus
\bm{\rho}=\bm{\rho}^{*}(t)
by continuity of
\bm{\rho}^{*}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

In the following theorem, we prove that along some further subsequence, we have
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}\to\bm{\rho}^{*}
in some stronger topology than proven in the previous lemma, which in combination with Corollary
4.11
allows us to show that the limit curve
\bm{\rho}^{*}
satisfies the weak formulation of the system of evolution equations stated in Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\bm{\rho}^{*}:[0,+\infty)\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
be the limit curve constructed in the previous lemma. There exists a further subsequence
\tau\downarrow 0
along which

(65)

\begin{split}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\begin{array}[]{c}\rho_{j}^{\tau}\to\rho_{j}^{*}\\[4.30554pt]
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\to F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{*})\end{array}}}\right\}\quad\text{ strongly in }L^{2}_{loc}([0,+\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}),\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\begin{array}[]{c}\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\rightharpoonup\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{*})\\[4.30554pt]
\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{\tau},\rho_{2}^{\tau})\rightharpoonup\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{*},\rho_{2}^{*})\\[4.30554pt]
\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{\tau}\rightharpoonup\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{*}\end{array}}}\right\}\quad\text{ weakly in }L^{2}_{loc}([0,+\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}).\end{split}

The limit curve
\bm{\rho}^{*}
is a weak solution to the system of evolution PDEs (
1
). More precisely, for every
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,+\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, it holds

(66)

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}^{*}\,\partial_{t}\zeta-\rho_{1}^{*}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{*})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\rho_{1}^{*},\rho_{2}^{*})+K\ast\rho_{2}^{*}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}\zeta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x&=0\\
\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{2}^{*}\,\partial_{t}\zeta-\rho_{2}^{*}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2}^{*})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\rho_{1}^{*},\rho_{2}^{*})+K\ast\rho_{1}^{*}}}\right]\cdot\nabla\zeta}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{2}^{0}\zeta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x&=0.\end{split}

Proof.

We fix some
T>0
and some smooth open bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and prove the existence of a subsequence
\tau\downarrow 0
such that the set of convergences (
65
) holds in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)
. Since
T
and
\Omega
are arbitrary, the
L^{2}_{loc}
-convergence then follows directly.

We start by integrating (
61
) from
0
to
T
to obtain a
\tau
-independent
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)
-bound on
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})
. With
N=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil T/\tau-1}}\right\rceil
, we have by non-negativity of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
and the inequality (
60
):

(67)

\begin{split}&\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\leq\sum_{n=0}^{N}\int_{n\tau}^{(n+1)\tau}\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j,n}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\sum_{n=0}^{N}\tau C\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})}{\tau}+\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})+\frac{\mathcal{H}_{c}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})-\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})}{\tau}+2dC_{K}}}\right)\\
&\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N+1}}\right)\tau\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})+\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{0})-\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})+2dC_{K}\tau}}\right)\\
&\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+(T+\tau)\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}}}\right)\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})+\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{0})+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1,n}^{+}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2,n}^{+}]-2+2\pi^{d/2}+2dC_{K}}}\right)\\
&\leq\hat{C}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})+\mathcal{H}_{c}(\bm{\rho}^{0})+1}}\right)\end{split}

for some constant
\hat{C}
independent of
\tau>0
sufficiently small. At the end, we have used
{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{1,n}^{+}]+{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{2,n}^{+}]\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})
, which follows from (
23
) and (
25
).

The Poincaré inequality in
\Omega
yields a
\tau
-independent constant
C_{\Omega}
such that

(68)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\Omega}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\Omega}F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)^{2}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t.

The first integral on the right is uniformly bounded in
\tau
as seen above, while the second one is uniformly bounded by using (
24
) and the uniform bound
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t))\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})
.
Hence, the whole expression on the right-hand side of (
68
) is uniformly bounded in
\tau
. Together, (
67
) and (
68
) imply existence of a
\tau
-independent constant
M<+\infty
such that

(69)

\int_{0}^{T}\|F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq M

for all
\tau\in(0,\bar{\tau}]
for some
\bar{\tau}>0
. We shall use this estimate together with the generalized version
[
18
, Theorem 2]
of the Aubin-Lions theorem to prove existence of a subsequence
\tau\downarrow 0
along which
\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\to\rho_{j}^{*}(t)
strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
at almost every
t\in[0,T]
. Specifically, we construct maps
\mathcal{F}:L^{2}(\Omega)\to[0,+\infty]
and
g:L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega)\to[0,+\infty]
such that
\mathcal{F}
is lower semicontinuous and coercive and
g
is lower semicontinuous, both with respect to the strong topology on
L^{2}(\Omega)
, and it holds

(70)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t\leq M\quad\text{ for all }\tau\in(0,\bar{\tau}],

(71)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\sup_{\tau\in(0,\bar{\tau}]}\int_{0}^{T-h}g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t+h),\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Since (
69
) and the at least linear growth of
F_{j}^{\prime}
imply
\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\in L^{2}(\Omega)
at almost every
t\in[0,T]
, the expressions in (
70
) and (
71
) are well-defined for every
\tau\in(0,\bar{\tau}]
. We now claim that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(u)

\displaystyle:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\|F_{j}^{\prime}(u)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}&\text{ if }F_{j}^{\prime}(u)\in H^{1}(\Omega)\\
+\infty&\text{ otherwise}\end{array}}}\right.,

\displaystyle g(u,v)

\displaystyle:=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathbf{W}}(\rho,\eta)^{2}:\rho,\eta\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}}),\ {\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho],{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\eta]\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0}),\ \rho|_{\Omega}=u,\eta|_{\Omega}=v}}\right\}

fulfill all desired properties. (
70
) is just (
69
) by definition of
\mathcal{F}
. To prove (
71
), we use that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{j}^{\tau}(s)]\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})
for all
\tau>0
and
s\geq 0
. By definition of
g
, this implies

\displaystyle g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t+h),\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)\leq{\mathbf{W}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t+h),\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)^{2}\leq\mathrm{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t+h),\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)^{2}

for all
h>0
and
t\in[0,T-h]
. Together with estimate (
64
), this yields for every fixed
\hat{\tau}\in(0,\bar{\tau}]
the estimate

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in(0,\hat{\tau}]}\int_{0}^{T-h}g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t+h),\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\sup_{\tau\in(0,\hat{\tau}]}\int_{0}^{T-h}\mathrm{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t+h),\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq 2T\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})(h+\hat{\tau})

Additionally, we prove that for every
\hat{\tau}\in(0,\bar{\tau}]
, it holds

\displaystyle\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\sup_{\tau\in[\hat{\tau},\bar{\tau}]}\int_{0}^{T-h}g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t+h),\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Indeed, for every
\tau\in[\hat{\tau},\bar{\tau}]
and every
h<\hat{\tau}
, it follows from the fact that
\rho_{j}^{\tau}
is constant on intervals of length
\tau>h
and inequality (
36
) that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-h}g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t+h),\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil T/\tau}}\right\rceil}h\mathrm{d}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n},\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})^{2}\leq 2\tau h\sum_{n=1}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil T/\tau}}\right\rceil}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})}}\right)\leq 2\bar{\tau}h\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0}).

Together, these two results yield (
71
), since
\hat{\tau}
can be arbitrarily small:

\displaystyle\limsup_{h\downarrow 0}\sup_{\tau\in(0,\bar{\tau}]}\int_{0}^{T-h}g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t+h),\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\lim_{h\downarrow 0}2T\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})(h+\hat{\tau})=2T\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})\hat{\tau}.

In order to prove lower semicontinuity of
g
with respect to strong
L^{2}(\Omega)
-convergence, and compatibility of
g
in the sense that
g(u,v)=0\ \Rightarrow\ u=v
for
u,v\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, we first observe that the infimum in the definition of
g
is attained as a minimum, unless
g(u,v)=+\infty
. To see this, note that in this case, the set

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\rho,\eta\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}}):\ {\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho],{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\eta]\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0}),\ \rho|_{\Omega}=u,\eta|_{\Omega}=v}}\right\}

is nonempty and compact with respect to narrow convergence, since pre-compactness follows from the bound on the second moments of
\rho
and
\eta
, and closedness from the fact that
\Omega
is an open set and the map
\rho\mapsto{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho]
is lower semicontinuous with respect to narrow convergence. This implies attainment of the minimum by lower semicontinuity of the Wasserstein distance in the narrow topology.

With this observation, the property
g(u,v)=0\ \Rightarrow\ u=v
is clear. To prove lower semicontinuity, let
u_{n}\to u
and
v_{n}\to v
be two convergent sequences in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Without loss of generality, we can assume
g(u_{n},v_{n})<+\infty
for all
n
and
g(u_{n},v_{n})\to C
with some
C<+\infty
. Hence for every
n
, there are
\rho_{n},\eta_{n}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
such that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{n}],{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\eta_{n}]\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})
,
\rho_{n}|_{\Omega}=u_{n}
,
\eta_{n}|_{\Omega}=v_{n}
and
g(u_{n},v_{n})={\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{n},\eta_{n})^{2}
. By the same compactness argument as above, it holds
\rho_{n}\to\rho
and
\eta_{n}\to\eta
narrowly up to subsequence, where
\rho
and
\eta
are admissible in the minimization problem in the definition of
g(u,v)
. Hence

\displaystyle g(u,v)\leq{\mathbf{W}}(\rho,\eta)^{2}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{n},\eta_{n})^{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}g(u_{n},v_{n})=C,

proving lower semicontinuity of
g
.

To show coercivity of the functional
\mathcal{F}
, observe that for any sequence
(u_{n})\subset L^{2}(\Omega)
along which
\mathcal{F}(u_{n})
is uniformly bounded for all
n
, the sequence
(F_{j}^{\prime}(u_{n}))
is weakly compact in
H^{1}(\Omega)
, hence by Rellich’s theorem,
F_{j}^{\prime}(u_{n})\to v
strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
up to subsequence. This implies
u_{n}\to u:=(F_{j}^{\prime})^{-1}(v)
strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
along the same subsequence by the at least linear growth of
F_{j}^{\prime}(r)
for large
r
, as seen in the proof of Theorem
4.9
, thus showing compactness of sublevel sets of
\mathcal{F}
.

In order to prove lower semicontinuity for
\mathcal{F}
, let
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Since without loss of generality, we can assume that
(\mathcal{F}(u_{n}))
is bounded, we again have that
(F_{j}^{\prime}(u_{n}))
is bounded and thus weakly compact in
H^{1}(\Omega)
. Together with the
L^{2}
-convergence
u_{n}\to u
, this implies
F_{j}^{\prime}(u_{n})\rightharpoonup F_{j}^{\prime}(u)
weakly in
H^{1}(\Omega)
. The claim thus follows from lower semicontinuity of the norm with respect to weak convergence.

All together, this shows that we can apply
[
18
, Theorem 2]
to the sequence of functions
\rho_{j}^{\tau}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
to obtain that along a subsequence
\tau\downarrow 0
, it holds
\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\to\rho_{j}^{*}(t)
strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
at almost every
t\geq 0
.

In order to get from this the claimed convergence
\rho_{j}^{\tau}\to\rho_{j}^{*}
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)
, which is equivalent to
\rho_{j}^{\tau}\to\rho_{j}^{*}
strongly in
L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
, we additionally show that the family of functions
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\|F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
is equi-integrable. It follows from the Sobolev embedding theorem that there exists an exponent
2^{*}>2
and a constant
C_{\Omega}
such that
\|u\|_{L^{2^{*}}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\Omega}\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}
for all
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. We denote

\displaystyle r:=\frac{2^{*}}{2}>1,\quad r^{\prime}:=\frac{r}{r-1},\quad q:=\frac{3r-1}{2r}>1.

Notice that
2(q-1)r^{\prime}=1
. We use Hölder twice to obtain at any
t\geq 0
with
u:=F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t))
:

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2q}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\Omega}1\cdot u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)^{q}\leq|\Omega|^{q-1}\int_{\Omega}u^{2q}\,\mathrm{d}x=|\Omega|^{q-1}\int_{\Omega}u^{2}u^{2(q-1)}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq|\Omega|^{q-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\Omega}u^{2r}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\Omega}u^{2(q-1)r^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)^{\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}}=|\Omega|^{q-1}\|u\|_{L^{2^{*}}(\Omega)}^{2}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq|\Omega|^{q-1}C_{\Omega}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha_{j}(1+2\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0}))}}\right)^{\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}}\,\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}=:C\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}.

In the last estimate, we used the Sobolev inequality and (
24
). With (
69
), this yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2q}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq CM

for all
\tau>0
. Since
q>1
, this implies equi-integrability of
\|F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
which also implies equi-integrability of
\|\rho_{j}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
by the at least linear growth of
F_{j}^{\prime}
. Together with the pointwise almost everywhere convergence
\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\to\rho_{j}^{*}(t)
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, Vitali’s convergence theorem thus yields
\rho_{j}^{\tau}\to\rho_{j}^{*}
strongly in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)
.

The convergence
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\to F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{*})
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)
also follows immediately, since pointwise almost everywhere convergence along a subsequence follows from continuity of
F_{j}^{\prime}
and the convergence
\rho_{j}^{\tau}\to\rho_{j}^{*}
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)
, and equi-integrability has been shown above.

By the bound (
67
), the sequence
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})
is uniformly bounded in
\tau
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega;{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. Alaoglu’s theorem thus yields
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\rightharpoonup v\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega;{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
weakly. Now it holds for every
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T]\times\Omega;{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, using the
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)
-convergence
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\to F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{*})
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{*})\ \mathrm{div}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=\lim_{\tau}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\ \mathrm{div}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\lim_{\tau}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\cdot\psi\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}v\cdot\psi\,\mathrm{d}x,

implying
v=\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})
.

The weak
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)
-convergence
\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{\tau},\rho_{2}^{\tau})\rightharpoonup\nabla\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{*},\rho_{2}^{*})
follows from the strong
L^{2}
-convergence
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{\tau})\to F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{*})
and assumption (
15
) by the same proof as in Theorem
4.9
. It thus remains to prove
\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{\tau}\rightharpoonup\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{*}
weakly in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega;{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. Let
\varphi\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega;{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
be arbitrary. Again as in the proof of Theorem
4.9
, the narrow convergence
\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\to\rho_{j}^{*}(t)
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
at every
t
and boundedness of the second moments imply

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\varphi_{t}\cdot\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x\to\int_{\Omega}\varphi_{t}\cdot\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{*}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x

at every
t\in[0,T]
where
\varphi_{t}=\varphi(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, which holds at almost every
t
. The estimates
|\nabla K(x-y)|^{2}\leq C_{K}^{2}|x-y|^{2}\leq 2C_{K}^{2}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})
and
{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}[\rho_{j}^{\tau}]\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})
further yield

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\Omega}\varphi_{t}\cdot\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{\tau}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla K(x-y)}}\right|^{2}\rho_{j}^{\tau}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\sqrt{2}\,C_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\lambda}|\Omega|\,\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})+\int_{\Omega}|x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

which is integrable from
0
to
T
since
\varphi\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)
. Hence by dominated convergence

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\varphi\cdot\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\to\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\varphi\cdot\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{*}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t,

proving weak
L^{2}
-convergence
\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{\tau}\rightharpoonup\nabla K\ast\rho_{j}^{*}
in
[0,T]\times\Omega
.

The claim that
\bm{\rho}^{*}
solves the weak form (
66
) of the evolution equations now follows directly from the set of convergences (
65
) and Corollary
4.11
: By the regularity of
\zeta
, it holds
\nabla\zeta^{\tau}\to\nabla\zeta
uniformly in
[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and since
\zeta
has compact support in
[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, the local convergences shown in (
65
) are sufficient to prove convergence of the integrals on the left-hand side of (
63
).
∎

5.
Exponential convergence to equilibrium

In this section, we prove that the weak solution
\bm{\rho}^{*}
to (
1
) constructed above converges exponentially in time to a global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}=(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
of the energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
. En passant, it follows that this minimizer is unique.

As explained in the introduction, we split the energy as follows:

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})+\varepsilon\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})

with functionals
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
given in (
9
) and (
10
), respectively. For the definition of the auxiliary potentials
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
by (
11
), any of the a priori many minimizers
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
can be used.
We shall prove that
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is geodesically
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
-convex with some
0<\lambda_{\varepsilon}<\lambda
, and that
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
is its unique global minimizer over
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. Furthermore, we show that it defines a Lyapunov functional for the minimizing movement scheme from the previous section, which will lead to exponential convergence to the equilibrium.

5.1.
Geodesic convexity of the auxiliary functional

We start by proving geodesic
\lambda
-convexity of the interaction energy.

Lemma 5.1
.

The interaction energy
\bm{\rho}\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
is geodesically
\lambda
-convex on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
.

Proof.

This is a special case of Zinsl’s result
[
24
]
on uniform geodesic convexity of
n
-component interaction functionals. In the case at hand, the proof simplifies as follows:

Take any
\bm{\rho}^{0},\bm{\rho}^{1}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. For simplicity, we assume that
\bm{\rho}^{0}
and
\bm{\rho}^{1}
are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. The general case then follows by density arguments and weak lower semi-continuity of the interaction energy, see e.g.
[
2
, Prop. 9.2.10]
. Denote by
T_{j}
the optimal transport maps pushing
\rho^{0}_{j}
to
\rho^{1}_{j}
, and denote by
(\bm{\rho}^{s})_{0\leq s\leq 1}
the geodesic between
\bm{\rho}^{0}
and
\bm{\rho}^{1}
, given by
\rho^{s}_{j}=T_{j}^{s}\#\rho_{j}^{0}
with
T_{j}^{s}=(1-s)\mathrm{id}+sT_{j}
. From the properties of the push-forward and
\lambda
-convexity of
K
, we obtain for the interaction energy of
\bm{\rho}^{s}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{s}_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}^{s}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}(x)\rho_{2}^{0}(y)\,K\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{1}^{s}(x)-T_{2}^{s}(y)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y
