\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}(x)\rho_{2}^{0}(y)\,K\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-s)(x-y)+s(T_{1}(x)-T_{2}(y)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq(1-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}K\ast\rho_{2}^{0}\,\mathrm{d}x+s\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{1}K\ast\rho_{2}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda}{2}s(1-s)\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}(x)\rho_{2}^{0}(y)\,|x-y-T_{1}(x)+T_{2}(y)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

For the last integral,
{\mathbf{W}}(\rho^{0}_{j},\rho^{1}_{j})^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{0}\,|x-T_{j}(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
and
\bm{\rho}^{0},\bm{\rho}^{1}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
yield

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}(x)\rho_{2}^{0}(y)\,|x-y-T_{1}(x)+T_{2}(y)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{0},\bm{\rho}^{1})^{2}-2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}(x)\,(x-T_{1}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{2}^{0}(y)\,(y-T_{2}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{0},\bm{\rho}^{1})^{2}-2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}^{0}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}^{1}]}}\right)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}^{0}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{2}^{1}]}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{0},\bm{\rho}^{1})^{2}+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}^{0}]-{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\rho_{1}^{1}]}}\right|^{2}\geq\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{0},\bm{\rho}^{1})^{2}.

Inserting above, this proves the inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{s}_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}^{s}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq(1-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}K\ast\rho_{2}^{0}\,\mathrm{d}x+s\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{1}K\ast\rho_{2}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}x-\frac{\lambda}{2}s(1-s)\,\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{0},\bm{\rho}^{1})^{2},

which is geodesic
\lambda
-convexity of the interaction energy.
∎

We assume in all of the following that
\varepsilon
is
small enough such that
\varepsilon K_{0}<\lambda
with the constant
K_{0}
from Corollary
3.12
.

Corollary 5.2
.

The functional
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
-convex along geodesics in
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
with the positive modulus
\lambda_{\varepsilon}=\lambda-\varepsilon K_{0}
.

Proof.

The
F
-terms are geodesically convex, since the
F_{j}
satisfy the McCann condition (
14
). Since
V_{j}
is
(-K_{0})
-semiconvex in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, the functionals
\varepsilon\int\rho_{j}V_{j}
are geodesically
(-\varepsilon K_{0})
-semiconvex, which then holds for their sum as well, since they only depend on one of the components each. Together with the previous lemma, this implies the
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
-convexity of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
in
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
.
∎

As a direct consequence of geodesic
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
-convexity, we prove the following technical inequality for
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
:

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\bm{\rho}^{0},\bm{\rho}^{1}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
be pairs and assume that both components of
\bm{\rho}^{0}
are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Additionally, we assume that
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{0})
exists almost everywhere in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and lies in
L^{2}(\mathrm{d}\rho_{j}^{0})
. Then, denoting by
T_{j}
the (unique) optimal transport maps pushing
\rho_{j}^{0}
to
\rho_{j}^{1}
, it holds

(72)

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{1})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})&\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{0})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}^{0}}}\right]\cdot(T_{1}(x)-x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{2}^{0}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2}^{0})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\rho_{1}^{0}}}\right]\cdot(T_{2}(x)-x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}{2}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{1},\bm{\rho}^{0})^{2}.\end{split}

Proof.

Denote by
(\bm{\rho}^{s})_{0\leq s\leq 1}
the geodesic between
\bm{\rho}^{0}
and
\bm{\rho}^{1}
, as in the proof of Lemma
5.1
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is geodesically
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
-convex, there holds the above-tangent formula

(73)

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{1})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}s}}}\right|_{s=0^{+}}^{+}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{s})+\frac{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}{2}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{1},\bm{\rho}^{0})^{2},

see
[
21
, Proposition 5.29]
. To compute the derivative, we use
[
21
, Theorem 5.30]
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}s}}}\right|_{s=0^{+}}^{+}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{s}V_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}\,\nabla V_{j}\cdot(T_{j}(x)-x)\,\mathrm{d}x,

using that the
V_{j}
are
(-K_{0})
-semiconvex in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. For the
F_{j}
-terms, we apply
[
2
, Theorem 10.4.6]
: Using our assumption
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{0})\in L^{2}(\mathrm{d}\rho_{j}^{0})
, it follows that
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{0})
lies in the subdifferential of the geodesically convex functional
\rho_{j}\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{j}(\rho_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x
at
\rho_{j}^{0}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}s}}}\right|_{s=0^{+}}^{+}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{j}(\rho_{j}^{s})\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{0}\,\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{0})\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{j}(x)-x}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x.

For the interaction term, we use
\rho_{j}^{s}=T_{j}^{s}\#\rho_{j}^{0}
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}^{s}K\ast\rho_{2}^{s}-\rho_{1}^{0}K\ast\rho_{2}^{0}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}(x)\rho_{2}^{0}(y)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[K\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{1}^{s}(x)-T_{2}^{s}(y)}}\right)-K(x-y)}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}(x)\rho_{2}^{0}(y)\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,K\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{1}^{st}(x)-T_{2}^{st}(y)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=s\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}(x)\rho_{2}^{0}(y)\int_{0}^{1}\nabla K\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{1}^{st}(x)-T_{2}^{st}(y)}}\right)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{1}(x)-x+y-T_{2}(y)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

To apply dominated convergence after dividing by
s
, observe that

\displaystyle|\nabla K\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{1}^{st}(x)-T_{2}^{st}(y)}}\right)|\leq C_{K}|T_{1}^{st}(x)-T_{2}^{st}(y)|\leq C_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|x-y|+|T_{1}(x)-x|+|T_{2}(y)-y|}}\right)

for every
s
and
t
, and that
\iint\rho_{1}^{0}\rho_{2}^{0}\,|T_{j}-\mathrm{id}|^{2}\leq\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho}^{0},\bm{\rho}^{1})^{2}<+\infty
, and that the
\rho_{j}^{0}
have bounded second moments. Dominated convergence and the continuity of
\nabla K
thus yield

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}s}}}\right|_{s=0^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{s}K\ast\rho_{2}^{s}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}(x)\rho_{2}^{0}(y)\nabla K(x-y)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{1}(x)-x+y-T_{2}(y)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}^{0}\,\nabla K\ast\rho_{2}^{0}\cdot(T_{1}(x)-x)+\rho_{2}^{0}\,\nabla K\ast\rho_{1}^{0}\cdot(T_{2}(x)-x)}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x.

Inserting these derivatives into (
73
) yields (
72
).
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

With the global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
chosen in the construction of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
, every
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
satisfies the inequality

(74)

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\geq\frac{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}{2}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho},\bar{\bm{\rho}})^{2}.

In particular,
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
is the unique global minimizer of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
over
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
.

Proof.

Take any
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. We apply the previous lemma with
\bm{\rho}^{0}=\bar{\bm{\rho}}
and
\bm{\rho}^{1}=\bm{\rho}
. Note that by Lemma
3.11
,
F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})
is globally Lipschitz, so in particular
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})\in L^{2}(\mathrm{d}\bar{\rho}_{j})
, hence the previous lemma is applicable. By Lemma
3.6
, the gradient of
F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{j}}h(\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2})+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}}
vanishes on the set
\{\bar{\rho}_{j}>0\}
, hence the integrals in (
72
) vanish, yielding (
74
). The second claim follows immediately by non-negativity of the distance.
∎

In order to analyze the functionals
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
further, we derive the following representation for the difference
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})
:

Lemma 5.5
.

For any
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
which is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, it holds

(75)

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})=\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho})+\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho})+\mathcal{K}(\bm{\rho})

with the three functionals

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(\rho_{1}|\bar{\rho}_{1})+\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}(\rho_{2}|\bar{\rho}_{2})]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})_{+}\,\rho_{1}+(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}-C_{2})_{+}\,\rho_{2}]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\bm{\rho}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\rho_{1}-\bar{\rho}_{1})K\ast(\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{2})\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.

From the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
and the fact that
\varepsilon V_{j}=(C_{j}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}})_{+}-F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})
by the Euler-Lagrange system (
28
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[F_{1}(\rho_{1})-F_{1}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+F_{2}(\rho_{2})-F_{2}(\bar{\rho}_{2})+(\rho_{1}-\bar{\rho}_{1})\varepsilon V_{1}+(\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{2})\varepsilon V_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{1}K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(\rho_{1}|\bar{\rho}_{1})+\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}(\rho_{2}|\bar{\rho}_{2})

(76)

\displaystyle\quad+(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}(\rho_{1}-\bar{\rho}_{1})+(C_{2}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1})_{+}(\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{2})+\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{1}K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}]\,\mathrm{d}x.

Using the identity
(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}=(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})_{+}+C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}
, together with the equality of mass of
\rho_{1}
and
\bar{\rho}_{1}
, and the fact that
\bar{\rho}_{1}\equiv 0
in
\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}>C_{1}\}
by Lemma
3.7
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}(\rho_{1}-\bar{\rho}_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})_{+}(\rho_{1}-\bar{\rho}_{1})+(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})(\rho_{1}-\bar{\rho}_{1})}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})_{+}\rho_{1}-\rho_{1}K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}+\bar{\rho}_{1}K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x

and similarly for
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(C_{2}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1})_{+}(\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{2})\,\mathrm{d}x
. Inserting into (
76
) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(\rho_{1}|\bar{\rho}_{1})+\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}(\rho_{2}|\bar{\rho}_{2})+(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})_{+}\rho_{1}+(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}-C_{2})_{+}\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\rho_{1}K\ast\rho_{2}+\bar{\rho}_{1}K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-\rho_{1}K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-\rho_{2}K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho})+\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho})+\mathcal{K}(\bm{\rho}),

proving the claim.
∎

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho})
is always non-negative by convexity of
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, and the same is true for
\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho})
. However, if
K
is not the quadratic potential
\frac{\lambda}{2}|\cdot|^{2}
, the term
\mathcal{K}(\bm{\rho})
can be negative. However, there hold the following general estimates:

Lemma 5.6
.

Take any
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. There holds the pointwise estimate

(77)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[K\ast(\bar{\rho}_{j}-\rho_{j})}}\right]}}\right|\leq C_{K}\,{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{j},\bar{\rho}_{j})

for
j=1,2

everywhere in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. The functional
\mathcal{K}
from the previous lemma satisfies

(78)

\mathcal{K}(\bm{\rho})\geq-\frac{C_{K}}{2}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho},\bar{\bm{\rho}})^{2}.

Moreover, it holds

(79)

\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho})+\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho})\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{C_{K}}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}}}\right)(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})).

Proof.

To prove (
77
), let
T:{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
be the optimal map transporting
\bar{\rho}_{j}
to
\rho_{j}
, given by Brenier’s Theorem. This allows us to write, fixing some
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
:

\displaystyle\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[K\ast(\bar{\rho}_{j}-\rho_{j})}}\right](x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla K(x-y)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bar{\rho}_{j}(y)-\rho_{j}(y)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla K(x-y)\bar{\rho}_{j}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla K(x-y)(T\#\bar{\rho}_{j})(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\nabla K(x-y)-\nabla K(x-T(y))}}\right]\,\bar{\rho}_{j}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

The global estimate
\|\nabla^{2}K\|\leq C_{K}
implies
|\nabla K(x-y)-\nabla K(x-T(y))|\leq C_{K}|y-T(y)|
for every
y\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. Together with Jensen’s inequality, this yields

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[K\ast(\bar{\rho}_{j}-\rho_{j})}}\right](x)}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\nabla K(x-y)-\nabla K(x-T(y))}}\right]\,\bar{\rho}_{j}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla K(x-y)-\nabla K(x-T(y))}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\rho}_{j}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq C_{K}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|y-T(y)|^{2}\bar{\rho}_{j}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=C_{K}^{2}\,{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{j},\bar{\rho}_{j})^{2},

proving the claimed estimate (
77
). To prove (
78
), let
T_{1}
be an optimal transport map pushing
\bar{\rho}_{1}
to
\rho_{1}
. For any
s\in[0,1]
, denote
T_{s}(x):=(1-s)x+sT_{1}(x)
. Using
\rho_{1}=T_{1}\#\bar{\rho}_{1}
and Fubini, which is applicable by boundedness of the second moments of
\rho_{j}
and
\bar{\rho}_{j}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\rho_{1}(x)-\bar{\rho}_{1}(x))\,(\rho_{2}(y)-\bar{\rho}_{2}(y))\,K(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)\,(\rho_{2}(y)-\bar{\rho}_{2}(y))\,[K(T_{1}(x)-y)-K(x-y)]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)\,(\rho_{2}(y)-\bar{\rho}_{2}(y))\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{0}^{1}\nabla K(T_{s}(x)-y)\cdot(T_{1}(x)-x)\,\mathrm{d}s}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)\,(\rho_{2}(y)-\bar{\rho}_{2}(y))\,\nabla K(T_{s}(x)-y)\cdot(T_{1}(x)-x)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)\,\nabla[K\ast(\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{2})](T_{s}(x))\cdot(T_{1}(x)-x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s.

This integral can be estimated with the help of the pointwise estimate (
77
) for
\rho_{2}
:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{K}(\bm{\rho})|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla[K\ast(\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{2})](T_{s}(x))\cdot(T_{1}(x)-x)}}\right|\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2C_{K}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla[K\ast(\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{2})](T_{s}(x))}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C_{K}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|T_{1}(x)-x}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2C_{K}}C_{K}^{2}{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{2},\bar{\rho}_{2})^{2}+\frac{C_{K}}{2}{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{1},\bar{\rho}_{1})^{2}=\frac{C_{K}}{2}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho},\bar{\bm{\rho}})^{2},

implying in particular
\mathcal{K}(\bm{\rho})\geq-\frac{C_{K}}{2}\mathrm{d}(\bm{\rho},\bar{\bm{\rho}})^{2}
, which is (
78
).

Combining (
75
) with the estimates (
78
) and (
74
) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho})+\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho})=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{K}(\bm{\rho})\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{C_{K}}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}}}\right)(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})),

proving (
79
), thus concluding the proof.
∎

5.2.
Controlling the non-convex terms

The next goal, which is a main step in our proof of exponential convergence, is to prove the following proposition that estimates the influence of the non-convex terms
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

There are
\varepsilon
-independent constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for
j=1,2
and every sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
:

(80)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\partial_{r_{j}}h(\bm{\rho})-V_{j}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}}))\\
&+c_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\rho_{1}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{2}}}\right]\mathrm{d}x\end{split}

for all
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
with
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j})
locally Lipschitz.

Proof.

Fix any pair
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
as above, and write
\bm{u}=(U_{1},U_{2}):=(F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2}))
, and similarly
\bar{\bm{u}}:=(F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1}),F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2}))
. Note that
\partial_{r_{j}}h(\bm{\rho})=\theta_{j}(\bm{u})
inherits local Lipschitz-continuity from
F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j})
, since
\theta_{j}
is Lipschitz on
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
by (
15
). Recall also that
F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})
is Lipschitz on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
by Lemma
3.11
. Taking the gradient with respect to
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is thus well-defined almost everywhere for all functions considered. We have

\nabla\theta_{j}(\bm{u})=\sum_{i=1,2}\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u})\,\nabla U_{i}=\sum_{i=1,2}\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u})\,\nabla F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i}).

Hence, we can rewrite the left-hand side of (
80
):

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\partial_{r_{j}}h(\bm{\rho})-V_{j}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\theta_{j}(\bm{u})-\theta_{j}(\bar{\bm{u}})}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x

(81)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\sum_{i=1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u})\,\nabla F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i})-\theta_{j,i}(\bar{\bm{u}})\,\nabla F_{i}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{i})}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x.

We split this sum into eight parts as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u})\,\nabla F_{i}^{\prime}(\rho_{i})-\theta_{j,i}(\bar{\bm{u}})\,\nabla F_{i}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{i})}}\right]

\displaystyle=\theta_{j,1}(\bm{u})\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}}}\right]+\theta_{j,2}(\bm{u})\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\rho_{1}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{j,1}(\bm{u})\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[K\ast(\bar{\rho}_{2}-\rho_{2})}}\right]+\theta_{j,2}(\bm{u})\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[K\ast(\bar{\rho}_{1}-\rho_{1})}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\theta_{j,1}(\bm{u})\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}}}\right]-\theta_{j,2}(\bm{u})\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\theta_{j,1}(\bm{u})-\theta_{j,1}(\bar{\bm{u}})}}\right)\,\nabla F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\theta_{j,2}(\bm{u})-\theta_{j,2}(\bar{\bm{u}})}}\right)\,\nabla F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{2})

\displaystyle=:B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}+B_{22}+B_{31}+B_{32}+B_{41}+B_{42}

Inserting into (
81
), we obtain

(82)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\partial_{r_{j}}h(\bm{\rho})-V_{j}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\sum_{k=1}^{4}\sum_{l=1}^{2}B_{kl}}}\right|^{2}\rho_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 8\sum_{k=1}^{4}\sum_{l=1}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|B_{kl}|^{2}\rho_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x.

We shall prove the claim by showing that each of the eight summands in (
82
) can be controlled by the right-hand side of (
80
) separately. Explicitly, we need estimates of the form

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|B_{kl}|^{2}\rho_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}}))+c_{2}A(\bm{\rho})

with constants
c_{1},c_{2}
, where

A(\bm{\rho}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\rho_{1}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x.

We may assume
j=1
, the other case is analogous. First, we observe that the function
\theta^{2}_{1,1}(\bm{u})
is globally bounded on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
by
\kappa^{2}_{1,1}
according to (
15
), and thus

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|B_{11}|^{2}\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\kappa_{1,1}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\kappa_{1,1}^{2}A(\bm{\rho}).

In order to get a similar estimate for the
B_{12}
-term, we use again (
15
) to see that the inequality
\theta_{1,2}^{2}(\bm{u})\rho_{1}\leq\kappa_{1,2}^{2}\rho_{2}
holds in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, hence

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|B_{12}|^{2}\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\kappa_{1,2}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\rho_{1}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\rho_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\kappa_{1,2}^{2}A(\bm{\rho}).

For the
B_{21}
- and
B_{22}
-terms, we use again (
15
) and the estimates (
77
) and (
74
) to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|B_{21}|^{2}\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\theta_{1,1}(\bm{u})|^{2}|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[K\ast(\bar{\rho}_{2}-\rho_{2})}}\right]|^{2}\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{1,1}^{2}C_{K}^{2}{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{2},\bar{\rho}_{2})^{2}\leq\frac{2\kappa_{1,1}^{2}C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})).

The term
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|B_{22}|^{2}\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x
is estimated analogously, using the property
|\theta_{1,2}(\bm{u})|^{2}\rho_{1}\leq\kappa_{1,2}^{2}\rho_{2}
.

For
B_{31}
, we use that
F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}=(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}
by the Euler-Lagrange equation, yielding

\displaystyle|B_{31}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\theta_{1,1}(\bm{u})\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}=\theta_{1,1}^{2}(\bm{u})\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2})_{+}+K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\theta_{1,1}^{2}(\bm{u})\,|\nabla[(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})_{+}]|^{2}=\theta_{1,1}^{2}(\bm{u})\,|\nabla[K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1}]|^{2}\,\mathds{1}_{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}>C_{1}\}}.

The function
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1}
inside the gradient is
\lambda
-convex with its second derivative bounded by
C_{K}
. With
x_{0}:=\argmin_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}[K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)-C_{1}]
and
c_{0}:=C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x_{0})
, it thus holds

|\nabla[K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)-C_{1}]|^{2}\leq C_{K}^{2}|x-x_{0}|^{2}\leq\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}(x)-C_{1}+c_{0}).

Inserting above and using
(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})\,\mathds{1}_{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}>C_{1}\}}=(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})_{+}
yields

(83)

|B_{31}|^{2}\leq\theta_{1,1}^{2}(\bm{u})\,\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})_{+}+\theta_{1,1}^{2}(\bm{u})\,\frac{2C_{K}^{2}c_{0}}{\lambda}\,\mathds{1}_{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}>C_{1}\}}

Integrated against
\rho_{1}
, the first term is easily controlled by
\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho})
:

(84)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\theta_{1,1}^{2}(\bm{u})\,\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}-C_{1})_{+}\,\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{2C_{K}^{2}\kappa_{1,1}^{2}}{\lambda}\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho}).

In the second term, note that the constant
c_{0}
is non-negative, as otherwise
C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}
would be negative everywhere in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, contradicting
\{\bar{\rho}_{1}>0\}=\{C_{1}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}>0\}
from Lemma
3.7
. Using the fact that on
\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}>C_{1}\}
, it is
\bar{\rho}_{1}=0
and thus
\theta_{1,1}(\bar{\bm{u}})=0
by (
15
), and inequality (
22
) together with
\bar{\rho}_{1},\bar{\rho}_{2}\leq H_{0}
from Lemma
3.10
, yields

(85)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\theta^{2}_{1,1}(\bm{u})\,\frac{2C_{K}^{2}c_{0}}{\lambda}\,\mathds{1}_{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}>C_{1}\}}\,\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{2C_{K}^{2}c_{0}}{\lambda}\int_{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}>C_{1}\}}|\theta_{1,1}(\bm{u})-\theta_{1,1}(\bar{\bm{u}})|^{2}\,\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq\frac{2C_{K}^{2}c_{0}\beta_{H_{0}}}{\lambda}\int_{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{2}>C_{1}\}}[\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(\rho_{1}|\bar{\rho}_{1})+\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}(\rho_{2}|\bar{\rho}_{2})]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{2C_{K}^{2}c_{0}\beta_{H_{0}}}{\lambda}\,\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho}).\end{split}

The last inequality follows from the non-negativity of
\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}
and
\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}
.
Combining the estimates (
84
) and (
85
) yields with (
83
):

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|B_{31}|^{2}\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}\max\{\kappa_{1,1}^{2},c_{0}\beta_{H_{0}}\}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho})+\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho})}}\right).

With (
79
), this implies that up to a constant factor,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|B_{31}|^{2}\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x
can be bounded from above by
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})
, which is the claimed estimate. Note that by Lemma
3.10
, the constant
c_{0}\leq C_{1}
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded for small enough
\varepsilon
.

For the
B_{32}
-term, we have the estimate

|B_{32}|^{2}\leq\theta_{1,2}^{2}(\bm{u})\,\frac{2C_{K}^{2}}{\lambda}(K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}-C_{2})_{+}+\theta_{1,2}^{2}(\bm{u})\,\frac{2C_{K}^{2}\tilde{c}_{0}}{\lambda}\,\mathds{1}_{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}>C_{2}\}}

with
\tilde{c}_{0}=\max_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[C_{2}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{1}(x)}}\right]>0
, which is derived analogously to (
83
). Integrated against
\rho_{1}
, the two terms are now estimated like above, using
\theta^{2}_{1,2}(\bm{u})\rho_{1}\leq\rho_{2}
from (
15
), and
|\theta_{1,2}(\bm{u})-\theta_{1,2}(\bar{\bm{u}})|^{2}\,\rho_{1}\leq\beta_{H_{0}}[\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(\rho_{1}|\bar{\rho}_{1})+\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}(\rho_{2}|\bar{\rho}_{2})]
from (
22
).

The last two terms
B_{4l}
for
l=1,2
can be estimated using the Lipschitz-continuity of
F_{l}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{l})
from Lemma
3.11
, implying
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{l}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{l})}}\right|^{2}\leq L_{0}^{2}
with an
\varepsilon
-independent constant
L_{0}
. Together with (
22
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|B_{4l}|^{2}\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\theta_{1,l}(\bm{u})-\theta_{1,l}(\bar{\bm{u}})}}\right|^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla F_{l}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{l})}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq L_{0}^{2}\beta_{H_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(\rho_{1}|\bar{\rho}_{1})+\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}(\rho_{2}|\bar{\rho}_{2})]\,\mathrm{d}x=L_{0}^{2}\beta_{H_{0}}\,\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho}).

Together with (
79
) and the fact that
\mathcal{I}_{K}(\bm{\rho})
is non-negative, this yields an estimate of the desired form, finishing the proof.
∎

In addition to Proposition
5.7
, which formally controls the slope of
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
by the one of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
, we prove the following lemma, which estimates the non-convex functional
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
itself by
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

There is an
\varepsilon
-independent constant
C_{\mathcal{N}}>0
such that for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
and all
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathcal{N}}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon C_{\mathcal{N}})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\leq(1+\varepsilon C_{\mathcal{N}})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right).

Proof.

We may assume that both components of
\bm{\rho}
are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. From the definition of
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
and the potentials
V_{j}
, it follows

(86)

\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[h(\bm{\rho})-h(\bar{\bm{\rho}})-\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\bm{\rho}})(\rho_{1}-\bar{\rho}_{1})-\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\bm{\rho}})(\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{2})}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x.

We shall derive a pointwise estimate for this integrand by means of
\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}
and
\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}
. With
{\mathbf{r}}_{s}=(r_{1,s},r_{2,s})=({\mathbf{r}}+s(\bar{\mathbf{r}}-{\mathbf{r}}))
and
\bm{u}_{s}=(F_{1}^{\prime}(r_{1,s}),F_{2}^{\prime}(r_{2,s}))
, it holds for all
{\mathbf{r}},\bar{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
by Taylor’s formula for
h
:

(87)

\begin{split}&h({\mathbf{r}})-h(\bar{\mathbf{r}})-\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}h(\bar{\mathbf{r}})\cdot({\mathbf{r}}-\bar{\mathbf{r}})=\int_{0}^{1}(\bar{\mathbf{r}}-{\mathbf{r}})\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}^{2}h({\mathbf{r}}_{s})(\bar{\mathbf{r}}-{\mathbf{r}})\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}(\bar{\mathbf{r}}-{\mathbf{r}})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1,1}(\bm{u}_{s})&\theta_{1,2}(\bm{u}_{s})\\
\theta_{2,1}(\bm{u}_{s})&\theta_{2,2}(\bm{u}_{s})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1,s})&0\\
0&F_{2}^{\prime\prime}(r_{2,s})\end{pmatrix}(\bar{\mathbf{r}}-{\mathbf{r}})\,s\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

With assumption (
15
) and using that
\theta_{1,2}(\bm{u}_{s})F_{2}^{\prime\prime}(r_{2,s})=\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{2}}h({\mathbf{r}}_{s})=\theta_{2,1}(\bm{u}_{s})F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1,s})
and the non-negativity of
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}
, we can estimate

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\bar{\mathbf{r}}-{\mathbf{r}})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1,1}(\bm{u}_{s})&\theta_{1,2}(\bm{u}_{s})\\
\theta_{2,1}(\bm{u}_{s})&\theta_{2,2}(\bm{u}_{s})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1,s})&0\\
0&F_{2}^{\prime\prime}(r_{2,s})\end{pmatrix}(\bar{\mathbf{r}}-{\mathbf{r}})}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\theta_{j,j}(\bm{u}_{s})}}\right|(\bar{r}_{j}-r_{j})^{2}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r_{j,s})+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\theta_{2,1}(\bm{u}_{s})(\bar{r}_{1}-r_{1})(\bar{r}_{2}-r_{2})}}\right|F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1,s})

\displaystyle\leq(\kappa_{1,1}+\kappa_{2,1})(\bar{r}_{1}-r_{1})^{2}F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1,s})+(\kappa_{1,2}+\kappa_{2,2})(\bar{r}_{2}-r_{2})^{2}F_{2}^{\prime\prime}(r_{2,s})

\displaystyle\leq\kappa\,(\bar{\mathbf{r}}-{\mathbf{r}})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1,s})&0\\
0&F_{2}^{\prime\prime}(r_{2,s})\end{pmatrix}(\bar{\mathbf{r}}-{\mathbf{r}})

with the constant
\kappa=\max_{i=1,2}\{\kappa_{1,i}+\kappa_{2,i}\}
, at every
s\in[0,1]
. Integrated from 0 to 1, this implies with (
87
) and the same Taylor formula argument as above

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|h({\mathbf{r}})-h(\bar{\mathbf{r}})-\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}h(\bar{\mathbf{r}})\cdot({\mathbf{r}}-\bar{\mathbf{r}})}}\right|\leq\kappa\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(r_{1}|\bar{r}_{1})+\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}(r_{2}|\bar{r}_{2})}}\right)

for every
{\mathbf{r}},\bar{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. Hence with (
86
) and (
79
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right|\leq\kappa\,\mathcal{I}_{F}(\bm{\rho})\leq\kappa\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{C_{K}}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}}}\right)(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}}))\leq\kappa\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{2C_{K}}{\lambda}}}\right)(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})),

assuming that
\varepsilon
is small enough such that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}
.
This proves the first claimed inequality. The second claim follows directly from the first by the identity
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
.
∎

Next, we show that in the inequality
(
80
)
, the term
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}}))
can be controlled by the other term on the right hand side and thus can be omitted.

Corollary 5.9
.

Fix
\bar{\varepsilon}
small enough such that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}=\lambda-K_{0}\varepsilon\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
. Then
there exists an
\varepsilon
-independent constant
C
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
and all
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
with
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j})\in L^{2}(\mathrm{d}\rho_{j})
, it holds

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bm{\rho})-V_{1}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bm{\rho})-V_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\rho_{1}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\rho_{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Remark 5.10
.

Formally, this is the inequality

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}}{\delta\rho_{1}}}}\right|^{2}\rho_{1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}}{\delta\rho_{2}}}}\right|^{2}\rho_{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}}{\delta\rho_{1}}}}\right|^{2}\rho_{1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}}{\delta\rho_{2}}}}\right|^{2}\rho_{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x

for all
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
as above, which is expected to imply exponential convergence to equilibrium as demonstrated heuristically in the introduction.

Proof.

We use Lemma
5.3
with
\bm{\rho}^{0}=\bm{\rho}
and
\bm{\rho}^{1}=\bar{\bm{\rho}}
. Using that by definition of
T_{j}
, it holds
{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{j}^{1},\rho_{j}^{0})^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{0}\,|T_{j}(x)-x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
, we estimate the integrals on the right-hand side:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{0})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}^{0}}}\right]\cdot(T_{1}(x)-x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{0}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{0})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}^{0}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x-\frac{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}{2}{\mathbf{W}}(\rho_{j}^{1},\rho_{j}^{0})^{2}

Inserting into (
72
) and multiplying by
-1
yields

(88)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho_{1}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}+\rho_{2}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\rho_{1}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

We set
C:=2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{c_{1}}{\lambda}+c_{2}}}\right)
with
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
from (
80
). Observing that by assumption
\frac{1}{2\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}
, the claim now follows from Proposition
5.7
.
∎

As a consequence of this estimate, we prove uniqueness of the minimizer of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
for every sufficiently small
\varepsilon
, as claimed in Theorem
1.1
.

Corollary 5.11
.

Assuming that
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small, the global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
of the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
is unique.

Proof.

Fix some
\varepsilon\leq\bar{\varepsilon}
such that
\sqrt{C}\varepsilon<1
, with
\bar{\varepsilon},C
being the constants from the previous corollary. Fix a global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
and denote
V_{j}=\partial_{r_{j}}h(\bar{\bm{\rho}})
, as above. Now suppose
\bar{\bm{\eta}}=(\bar{\eta}_{1},\bar{\eta}_{2})\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
is another global minimizer. Applying Theorem
3.8
for
\bar{\bm{\eta}}
, we get the Euler-Lagrange system (
28
), which we rewrite as

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\bm{\eta}})

\displaystyle=F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{1})-(C_{1,\eta}-K\ast\bar{\eta}_{2})_{+}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\bm{\eta}})

\displaystyle=F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{2})-(C_{2,\eta}-K\ast\bar{\eta}_{1})_{+}

with constants
C_{j,\eta}
. Applying Corollary
5.9
with
\bm{\rho}=\bar{\bm{\eta}}
, which is possible as
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{j})
is globally bounded in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
by Lemma
3.11
, thus yields

\displaystyle C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\bar{\eta}_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\bar{\eta}_{1}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{2}}}\right]\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[V_{1}-\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\bm{\eta}})}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[V_{2}-\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\bm{\eta}})}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\varepsilon V_{1}-\varepsilon\partial_{r_{1}}h(\bar{\bm{\eta}})}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\varepsilon V_{2}-\varepsilon\partial_{r_{2}}h(\bar{\bm{\eta}})}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}-(C_{1,\eta}-K\ast\bar{\eta}_{2})_{+}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right.

\displaystyle\quad+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}-(C_{2,\eta}-K\ast\bar{\eta}_{1})_{+}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right]

\displaystyle=C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\bar{\eta}_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\bar{\eta}_{1}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{2}}}\right]\mathrm{d}x,

implying equality in every step.
The last equality follows from the fact that
C_{1,\eta}>K\ast\bar{\eta}_{2}
on
\{\bar{\eta}_{1}>0\}
and
C_{2,\eta}>K\ast\bar{\eta}_{1}
on
\{\bar{\eta}_{2}>0\}
. Since
C<\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}
by assumption, equality in the second-to-last step means that the sum of the integral terms must vanish, and thus every expression above is equal to 0. In particular,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{1})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\bar{\eta}_{2}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}_{2})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\bar{\eta}_{1}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\bar{\eta}_{2}}}\right]\mathrm{d}x=0.

Together with (
88
), this implies
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\eta}})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\leq 0
. Since
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
is the unique global minimizer of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
by Corollary
5.4
, this yields
\bar{\bm{\eta}}=\bar{\bm{\rho}}
.
∎

This uniqueness result allows us to prove additional properties of the minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
. In particular, we obtain the following symmetry result:

Lemma 5.12
.

Under the same assumptions on
\varepsilon
as in the previous corollary, both components
\bar{\rho}_{j}
of the unique global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
are radially symmetric around their center of mass
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}[\bar{\rho}_{j}]=0
.

Proof.

The claim will follow from rotational invariance of the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
. Let
R:{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
be any orthogonal transformation. Define the pair of probability densities
\bm{\rho}^{R}:=(\rho_{1}^{R},\rho_{2}^{R})
by
\rho_{j}^{R}(x):=\bar{\rho}_{j}(Rx)
. It holds
\bm{\rho}^{R}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{R})

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\rho}_{1}(Rx),\bar{\rho}_{2}(Ry))+\bar{\rho}_{1}(Rx)\bar{\rho}_{2}(Ry)K(x-y))\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\rho}_{1}(x^{\prime}),\bar{\rho}_{2}(y^{\prime}))+\bar{\rho}_{1}(x^{\prime})\bar{\rho}_{2}(y^{\prime})K(R^{T}x^{\prime}-R^{T}y^{\prime})}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}}),

where we have substituted
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(Rx,Ry)
. The last equality follows from the fact that
K(R^{T}x^{\prime}-R^{T}y^{\prime})=K(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})
by radial symmetry of the interaction potential. By uniqueness of the minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
, this implies
\bm{\rho}^{R}=\bar{\bm{\rho}}
. Since
R
was arbitrary, this yields radial symmetry of
\bar{\rho}_{j}
.
∎

5.3.
Exponential decay

We shall now use the estimates from Lemma
5.3
and Corollary
5.9
to prove a decay estimate for the functional
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
in the minimizing movement scheme for
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
, as analyzed in section
4
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\hat{\bm{\rho}}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{2}
be a pair such that
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})<+\infty
, and let
\bm{\rho}^{+}
be a global minimizer of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\tau}(\cdot|\hat{\bm{\rho}})
. Then with the constant
C
from Corollary
5.9
, there holds

(89)

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\geq 2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right).

Proof.

We first prove the estimate under the stronger assumption that
\hat{\rho}_{1}
and
\hat{\rho}_{2}
are strictly positive inside balls
B_{R_{1}}
and
B_{R_{2}}
and vanish almost everywhere outside, so that Lemma
4.3
and its consequences are applicable. In this case, it follows from Lemma
4.7
that
\nabla F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})\in L^{2}(\mathrm{d}\rho_{j}^{+})
, hence we can apply Lemma
5.3
to
\bm{\rho}^{0}=\bm{\rho}^{+}
and
\bm{\rho}^{1}=\hat{\bm{\rho}}
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{1}^{+}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{1}^{\prime}(\rho_{1}^{+})+\varepsilon V_{1}+K\ast\rho_{2}^{+}}}\right]\cdot(T_{1}(x)-x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{2}^{+}\,\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{2}^{\prime}(\rho_{2}^{+})+\varepsilon V_{2}+K\ast\rho_{1}^{+}}}\right]\cdot(T_{2}(x)-x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}{2}\mathrm{d}(\hat{\bm{\rho}},\bm{\rho}^{+})^{2}

with the optimal transport maps
T_{j}
pushing
\rho_{j}^{+}
to
\hat{\rho}_{j}
. For brevity, we denote
\mathcal{D}_{j}(\bm{\rho}):=F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j})+\varepsilon V_{j}+K\ast\rho_{j^{\prime}}
. Inserting (
47
) and omitting the non-negative distance term yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\geq\tau\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{+}\,\nabla\mathcal{D}_{j}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\cdot\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{j}^{\prime}(\rho_{j}^{+})+\varepsilon\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})+K\ast\rho_{j^{\prime}}^{+}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\tau\sum_{j=1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{+}\,\nabla\mathcal{D}_{j}(\bm{\rho}^{+})\cdot\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})-V_{j}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathcal{D}_{j}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\tau\sum_{j=1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{C}}{2}\varepsilon}}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathcal{D}_{j}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x-\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{C}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{+}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\partial_{r_{j}}h(\rho_{1}^{+},\rho_{2}^{+})-V_{j}}}\right]}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\tau\sum_{j=1,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{j}^{+}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\nabla\mathcal{D}_{j}(\bm{\rho}^{+})}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\geq 2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right),

where in the last two inequalities we used Corollary
5.9
and (
88
) with
\bm{\rho}=\bm{\rho}^{+}
, respectively.

In the case of a more general
\hat{\bm{\rho}}
, we use the approximating sequence of pairs
\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}
from the proof of Theorem
4.9
, which satisfy the stronger assumptions. Hence, we can apply the proven result to
\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})\geq 2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}_{n})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)+\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}_{n}).

It follows from the construction that
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})
. On the right-hand side, we use the narrow convergence
\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}\to\bm{\rho}^{+}
and lower semi-continuity of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
with respect to narrow convergence, which holds since the
V_{j}
are bounded and continuous. This proves the claim.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

To see why Lemma
5.13
yields the desired decay estimate, observe that by abbreviating the relative energy as
\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}):=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})
, (
89
) can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{r}(\bm{\rho}^{+})-\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}})}{\tau}\leq-2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{+}).

In the following theorem, we show that this estimate leads to exponential decay of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{r}
along the limit curve
\bm{\rho}^{*}
constructed in Lemma
4.13
, which thus can be viewed as a discrete version of Gronwall’s lemma.

Theorem 5.15
.

For any initial condition
\bm{\rho}^{0}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
with
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})
finite, the weak solution
\bm{\rho}^{*}:[0,+\infty)\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
to system (
1
) with
\bm{\rho}^{*}(0)=\bm{\rho}^{0}
from subsection
4.5
satisfies

(90)

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{*}(t))-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\leq e^{-2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)t}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)

at every
t\geq 0
. In particular, if
\varepsilon<1/\sqrt{C}
, the functional
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
decays at an exponential rate along the solution curve
\bm{\rho}^{*}
. If in addition,
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{C_{\mathcal{N}}}
with
C_{\mathcal{N}}
from Lemma
5.8
, it holds

(91)

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{*}(t))-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\leq\frac{1+\varepsilon C_{\mathcal{N}}}{1-\varepsilon C_{\mathcal{N}}}\,e^{-2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)t}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right),

at every
t\geq 0
, proving the exponential decay for
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
claimed in Theorem
1.1
.

Proof.

Fix any
\varepsilon>0
and consider the interpolation scheme (
62
). Applying Lemma
5.13
, we obtain for every
n
the estimate

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})\geq 2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)

After adding
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})
and assuming
\tau
is small enough such that
2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\tau(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon)>-1
, this becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)}}\right)^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right).

Using that
\bm{\rho}_{n-1}^{+}=\hat{\bm{\rho}}_{n}
for every
n\geq 1
, induction over
n
yields

(92)

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)}}\right)^{-(n+1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)

for every
n\geq 0
. Now fix
t>0
and
n
such that
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)=\bm{\rho}_{n}^{+}
, which holds for
n\tau<t\leq(n+1)\tau
. In particular, we have
n+1\geq t/\tau
, and thus with (
92
),

(93)

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)}}\right)-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)}}\right)^{-t/\tau}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)

In the limit
\tau\downarrow 0
, the right-hand side of (
93
) converges to the right-hand side of (
90
). On the left-hand side, note that
\bm{\rho}^{\tau}(t)\to\bm{\rho}^{*}(t)
narrowly by Lemma
4.13
and that
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is lower semi-continuous with respect to narrow convergence. This proves (
90
). The estimate (
91
) follows directly from (
90
) and Lemma
5.8
.
∎

Note that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon)=\lambda-(K_{0}+\sqrt{C})\varepsilon+K_{0}\sqrt{C}\varepsilon^{2}
, hence the exponential decay estimate (
6
) claimed in the introduction is satisfied for every sufficiently small
\varepsilon
with any constants
C_{0}>K_{0}+\sqrt{C}
and
C_{1}>1
.

In order to prove that this decay estimate implies exponential convergence of
\bm{\rho}^{*}
to
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
, we first need the following technical lemma, which estimates the behaviour of the
\bar{\rho}_{j}
near the boundary of their support.

Lemma 5.16
.

For
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small, there exists an
\varepsilon
-independent constant
A<+\infty
such that for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{\{\bar{\rho}_{j}>0\}}\frac{1}{F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq A.

Proof.

The proof is based on assumption (
13
) and the estimates from Lemma
3.10
. Recall that for
j=1,2
, it holds

\displaystyle(C_{j}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}})_{+}\leq(1+\varepsilon\kappa_{j,j})\,F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j}),

see (
32
). (
13
) implies that for a sufficiently small
r_{0}>0
, we have for every
r\in[0,r_{0}]
the estimate

\displaystyle M_{j}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})\geq\bar{\rho}_{j}^{\,\beta_{j}}\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m_{j}(\beta_{j}+1)}}\right)^{\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}}F_{j}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})^{\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}}\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{m_{j}(\beta_{j}+1)}{1+\varepsilon\kappa_{j,j}}}}\right)^{\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}}(C_{j}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}})_{+}^{\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}}

with the constants
m_{j},M_{j},\beta_{j}
from (
13
). The last estimate follows from (
32
). Since the constant in the last expression is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded for small
\varepsilon
, the claim will follow if we show that the value of the integral

\displaystyle\int_{\{\bar{\rho}_{j}>0\}}(C_{j}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}})_{+}^{-\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}}<C_{j}\}}(C_{j}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}})^{-\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}}\,\mathrm{d}x

is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded for small
\varepsilon
. Recall that by Lemma
5.12
,
\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}}
is radially symmetric, thus
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}}
, which is
\lambda
-convex, is radially increasing around 0. This implies that the set
\{\bar{\rho}_{j}>0\}=\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}}<C_{j}\}
is a ball
B_{R}
, whose radius
R
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded by Lemma
3.10
. Since the density
\bar{\rho}_{j}
is
\varepsilon
-uniformly bounded by
H_{0}
, again by Lemma
3.10
, the radius
R
is also uniformly bounded away from 0 for small
\varepsilon
. By
\lambda
-convexity and radial symmetry, this implies that the gradient of
K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}}
is uniformly bounded away from 0 near the boundary of
B_{R}
, implying that for some constant
c>0
, it holds
C_{j}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}}(x)\geq c\,(R-|x|)
at every
x\in B_{R}
, meaning we can estimate the integral above by

\displaystyle\int_{\{K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}}<C_{j}\}}(C_{j}-K\ast\bar{\rho}_{j^{\prime}})^{-\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq c^{-\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}}\int_{B_{R}}(R-|x|)^{-\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}}\,\mathrm{d}x,

and the integral on the right is finite since
0\leq\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{j}+1}<1
. This proves the claim.
∎

With the help of this estimate, we can prove the following version of the Csiszár-Kullback inequality for the functional
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
:

Lemma 5.17
.

There is an
\varepsilon
-independent constant
C_{CK}
such that for all
\bm{\rho}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
with
\rho_{j}
absolutely continuous w.r.t the Lebesgue measure and all
\varepsilon
such that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}
, it holds

\displaystyle\|\rho_{1}-\bar{\rho}_{1}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}^{2}+\|\rho_{2}-\bar{\rho}_{2}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}^{2}\leq C_{CK}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right).

Proof.

Inequality (
79
) and
\lambda_{\varepsilon}\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}
give the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{d}_{F_{1}}(\rho_{1}|\bar{\rho}_{1})+\mathrm{d}_{F_{2}}(\rho_{2}|\bar{\rho}_{2})}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{2C_{K}}{\lambda}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right),

thus the claim will follow if we show an estimate of the form

\displaystyle\|\rho_{j}-\bar{\rho}_{j}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}^{2}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}_{F_{j}}(\rho_{j}|\bar{\rho}_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x

with some
\varepsilon
-uniform constant
C
. To prove this, observe that

(94)

\|\rho_{j}-\bar{\rho}_{j}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}=\int_{\{\rho_{j}<\bar{\rho}_{j}\}}(\bar{\rho}_{j}-\rho_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\{\rho_{j}\geq\bar{\rho}_{j}\}}(\rho_{j}-\bar{\rho}_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x=2\int_{\{\rho_{j}<\bar{\rho}_{j}\}}(\bar{\rho}_{j}-\rho_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x,

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\rho_{j}
and
\bar{\rho}_{j}
have unit mass. Additionally, it follows from the non-negativity of
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}
that for every
0\leq r<\bar{r}
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{F_{j}}(r|\bar{r})

\displaystyle=\int_{r}^{\bar{r}}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(s)(s-r)\,\mathrm{d}s\geq\int_{\frac{r+\bar{r}}{2}}^{\bar{r}}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(s)(s-r)\,\mathrm{d}s\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\bar{r}-r}{2}}}\right)^{2}\min_{s\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{r+\bar{r}}{2},\bar{r}}}\right]}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(s).

The minimum on the right-hand side can be estimated further with assumption (
13
), as for every
s\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{r+\bar{r}}{2},\bar{r}}}\right]
, it holds
s\geq\frac{\bar{r}}{2}
and thus

\displaystyle F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(s)\geq\frac{1}{M_{j}}s^{\beta_{j}}\geq\frac{1}{M_{j}2^{\beta_{j}}}\,\bar{r}^{\beta_{j}}\geq\frac{m_{j}}{M_{j}2^{\beta_{j}}}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{r}).

Combining the last two inequalities yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{F_{j}}(r|\bar{r})\geq\frac{m_{j}}{M_{j}2^{\beta_{j}+2}}\,F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{r})(\bar{r}-r)^{2}.

With (
94
), the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, Lemma
5.16
and
\mathrm{d}_{F_{j}}(r|\bar{r})\geq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\rho_{j}-\bar{\rho}_{j}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}^{2}=4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\{\rho_{j}<\bar{\rho}_{j}\}}(\bar{\rho}_{j}-\rho_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\{\rho_{j}<\bar{\rho}_{j}\}}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})(\bar{\rho}_{j}-\rho_{j})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\{\rho_{j}<\bar{\rho}_{j}\}}\frac{1}{F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{j})}\,\mathrm{d}x}}\right)\leq\frac{M_{j}2^{\beta_{j}+4}A}{m_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}_{F_{j}}(\rho_{j}|\bar{\rho}_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x.

This is an inequality of the desired form given above, thus proving the claim.
∎

Exponential convergence to the equilibrium for the solution curve
\bm{\rho}^{*}
is now a direct consequence of the previous results:

Theorem 5.18
.

Assume that
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
is sufficiently small. Then given any
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
and any initial data
\bm{\rho}^{0}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}
with
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})<+\infty
, the weak solution
\bm{\rho}^{*}
to system (
1
) from subsection
4.5
satisfies at every
t\geq 0
the estimate

\displaystyle\|\rho_{1}^{*}(t)-\bar{\rho}_{1}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}^{2}+\|\rho_{2}^{*}(t)-\bar{\rho}_{2}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{CK}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)\,e^{-2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{CK}}{1-\varepsilon C_{\mathcal{N}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\rho}^{0})-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{\bm{\rho}})}}\right)\,e^{-2\lambda_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\sqrt{C}\varepsilon}}\right)t}

with the constants
C_{CK}
from the previous lemma and
C_{\mathcal{N}}
from Lemma
5.8
. In particular,
\bm{\rho}^{*}(t)
converges to the unique global minimizer
\bar{\bm{\rho}}
of the energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
at an exponential rate in
L^{1}
.

Proof.

The first inequality follows directly from Lemma
5.17
and Theorem
5.15
, while the second one follows from Lemma
5.8
.
∎

Appendix A
Example

In this section, we give a generic example of functions
F_{1},F_{2}
and
h
and prove that they satisfy all assumption from Hypotheses
2.1
and
2.2
under certain conditions. Note that the assumptions on
K
are independent from
F_{1},F_{2}
and
h
and vice versa.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2},\gamma
be positive real numbers. Consider

\displaystyle F_{1}(r):=\frac{1}{a_{1}}\,r^{a_{1}},\quad F_{2}(r):=\frac{1}{a_{2}}\,r^{a_{2}},\quad h({\mathbf{r}}):=\frac{r_{1}^{b_{1}}r_{2}^{b_{2}}}{(1+r_{1}+r_{2})^{\gamma}}.

Then the nonlinearities
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
and the coupling function
h
fulfill all the assumptions from Hypotheses
2.1
and
2.2
if and only if for
j=1,2
, it holds

(95)

a_{j}\geq 2,\qquad b_{j}\geq 2a_{j}-1,\qquad b_{1}+b_{2}\leq\gamma+\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}.

Proof.

We have
F_{j}^{\prime}(r)=r^{a_{j}-1},F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)=(a_{j}-1)r^{a_{j}-2}
. Hence assumption (
13
) is satisfied with
\beta_{j}=a_{j}-2
, which is non-negative if and only if
a_{j}\geq 2
. In this case, the McCann-condition (
14
) is always fulfilled, since

\displaystyle F_{j}(r)-rF_{j}^{\prime}(r)+r^{2}F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{a_{j}}-1+a_{j}-1}}\right)\,r^{a_{j}}\geq 0.

The other conditions on
F_{j}
from Hypothesis
2.1
are clearly satisfied as well. In order to verify the flatness assumptions on
h
, in particular Hypothesis
2.2
, we introduce the following notations: Given arbitrary real numbers
b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime}
, we define
Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
as

\displaystyle Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}({\mathbf{r}}):=\frac{r_{1}^{b_{1}^{\prime}}r_{2}^{b_{2}^{\prime}}}{(1+r_{1}+r_{2})^{\gamma^{\prime}}}.

Note that
h=Q_{b_{1},b_{2},\gamma}
. Observe that
Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}
is bounded in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
if and only if

(96)

b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}\geq 0,\quad b_{1}^{\prime}+b_{2}^{\prime}\leq\gamma^{\prime},

while for local boundedness, only the first condition is required. If
b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}>0
, the function
Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}
vanishes on
\partial{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
.

Additionally, for any functions
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we define
V(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{n})
to be the set of all linear combinations of the functions
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{n}
. It is clear that
Q\in V(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{n})
is (locally) bounded in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
if every
Q_{i}
is (locally) bounded for
i=1,\dots,n
, and that
Q
vanishes everywhere on
\partial{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
if every
Q_{i}
does. Now, observe that

(97)

\begin{split}\partial_{r_{1}}Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}=b_{1}^{\prime}\,Q_{b_{1}^{\prime}-1,b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}-\gamma^{\prime}\,Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}+1}\in V(Q_{b_{1}^{\prime}-1,b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}},Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}+1})\\
\partial_{r_{2}}Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}=b_{2}^{\prime}\,Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}-1,\gamma^{\prime}}-\gamma^{\prime}\,Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}+1}\in V(Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}-1,\gamma^{\prime}},Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}+1}).\end{split}

In follows that
\partial_{r_{1}}h\in V(Q_{b_{1}-1,b_{2},\gamma},Q_{b_{1},b_{2},\gamma+1})
and
\partial_{r_{2}}h\in V(Q_{b_{1},b_{2}-1,\gamma},Q_{b_{1},b_{2},\gamma+1})
. Hence, the assumption
\partial_{r_{j}}h({\mathbf{r}})=0
for every
{\mathbf{r}}\in\partial{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
from Hypothesis
2.1
is satisfied if and only if
b_{j}>1
for
j=1,2
. It thus remains to verify the assumptions from Hypothesis
2.2
. We start with condition (
16
). For the second derivatives of
h
, we obtain by applying (
97
) twice:

(98)

\begin{split}\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{1}}h&\in V(Q_{b_{1}-2,b_{2},\gamma},Q_{b_{1}-1,b_{2},\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1},b_{2},\gamma+2})\\
\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{2}}h&\in V(Q_{b_{1}-1,b_{2}-1,\gamma},Q_{b_{1}-1,b_{2},\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1},b_{2}-1,\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1},b_{2},\gamma+2})\\
\partial_{r_{2}}\partial_{r_{2}}h&\in V(Q_{b_{1},b_{2}-2,\gamma},Q_{b_{1},b_{2}-1,\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1},b_{2},\gamma+2}).\end{split}

In order to prove (
16
), note that for any
m_{1},m_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, it holds

(99)

\displaystyle r_{1}^{m_{1}}r_{2}^{m_{2}}Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}({\mathbf{r}})=Q_{b_{1}^{\prime}+m_{1},b_{2}^{\prime}+m_{2},\gamma^{\prime}}({\mathbf{r}}).

From this observation and
F_{j}^{\prime}(r)=r^{a_{j}-1}
,
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)=(a_{j}-1)r^{a_{j}-2}
, we obtain with (
98
)

(100)

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{1}}h({\mathbf{r}})}{F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})}\in V(Q_{b_{1}-a_{1},b_{2},\gamma},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+1,b_{2},\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+2,b_{2},\gamma+2})\\
&\frac{\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{1}}h({\mathbf{r}})}{F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})F_{1}^{\prime}(r_{1})}\in V(Q_{b_{1}-2a_{1}+1,b_{2},\gamma},Q_{b_{1}-2a_{1}+2,b_{2},\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-2a_{1}+3,b_{2},\gamma+2})\\
&\frac{\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{1}}h({\mathbf{r}})}{F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})F_{2}^{\prime}(r_{2})}\in V(Q_{b_{1}-a_{1},b_{2}-a_{2}+1,\gamma},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+1,b_{2}-a_{2}+1,\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+2,b_{2}-a_{2}+1,\gamma+2})\\
&\frac{\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{2}}h({\mathbf{r}})}{F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})}\in V(Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+1,b_{2}-1,\gamma},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+1,b_{2},\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+2,b_{2}-1,\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+2,b_{2},\gamma+2})\\
&\frac{\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{2}}h({\mathbf{r}})}{F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})F_{1}^{\prime}(r_{1})}\in V(Q_{b_{1}-2a_{1}+2,b_{2}-1,\gamma},Q_{b_{1}-2a_{1}+2,b_{2},\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-2a_{1}+3,b_{2}-1,\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-2a_{1}+3,b_{2},\gamma+2})\\
&\frac{\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{2}}h({\mathbf{r}})}{F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})F_{2}^{\prime}(r_{2})}\in V(Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+1,b_{2}-a_{2},\gamma},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+1,b_{2}-a_{2}+1,\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+2,b_{2}-a_{2},\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+2,b_{2}-a_{2}+1,\gamma+2})\\
&\frac{\partial_{r_{1}}\partial_{r_{2}}h({\mathbf{r}})\sqrt{r_{2}}}{F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(r_{1})\sqrt{r_{1}}}\in V(Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+\frac{1}{2},b_{2}-\frac{1}{2},\gamma},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+\frac{1}{2},b_{2}+\frac{1}{2},\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+\frac{3}{2},b_{2}-\frac{1}{2},\gamma+1},Q_{b_{1}-a_{1}+\frac{3}{2},b_{2}+\frac{1}{2},\gamma+2}).\end{split}

In order for inequality (
16
) to be true, we need all these functions to be bounded in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. All other functions that are assumed to be bounded by (
16
) are analogous to one of the functions appearing above, hence we obtain the set of conditions on
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2},\gamma
required to fulfill (
16
) by analyzing when all functions above are bounded. In order to get this boundedness, (
96
) needs to be satisfied for all
b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}
appearing above. The condition
b_{1}^{\prime}\geq 0
yields
b_{1}\geq 2a_{1}-1
, since
b_{1}-2a_{1}+1
appearing in the second line is the smallest
b_{1}^{\prime}
appearing above. By symmetry, this implies that
b_{2}\geq 2a_{2}-1
is needed too, which is sufficient for
b_{2}^{\prime}\geq 0
for all
b_{2}^{\prime}
above. For the inequality
b_{1}^{\prime}+b_{2}^{\prime}\leq\gamma^{\prime}
observe that in every line above, the value of
b_{1}^{\prime}+b_{2}^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime}
is the same for all
Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}
appearing in that line, thus we only need to analyze the first term. The largest value of
b_{1}^{\prime}+b_{2}^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime}
appearing above is
b_{1}-a_{1}+b_{2}-\gamma
in the first line, hence we require
b_{1}+b_{2}\leq\gamma+a_{1}
, and by symmetry,
b_{1}+b_{2}\leq\gamma+\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
. This shows that
F_{1},F_{2}
and
h
satisfy assumption (
16
) if and only if (
95
) holds.

It remains to show that (
95
) implies local Lipschitz-continuity of all
\theta_{j,i}
. It holds

\displaystyle\theta_{j,i}(\bm{u})=\frac{\partial_{r_{i}}\partial_{r_{j}}h({\mathbf{r}})}{F_{i}^{\prime\prime}(r_{i})}\ \text{ with }{\mathbf{r}}=(r_{1},r_{2})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u_{1}^{\frac{1}{a_{1}-1}},u_{2}^{\frac{1}{a_{2}-1}}}}\right).

We analyze under which condition a function of the form
\bm{u}\mapsto Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(u_{1}^{p_{1}},u_{2}^{p_{2}})
with positive exponents
p_{1},p_{2}
is locally Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. It holds by (
97
) and (
99
):

\displaystyle\partial_{u_{1}}Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(u_{1}^{p_{1}},u_{2}^{p_{2}})

\displaystyle=p_{1}u_{1}^{p_{1}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{1}^{\prime}Q_{b_{1}^{\prime}-1,b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(u_{1}^{p_{1}},u_{2}^{p_{2}})-\gamma^{\prime}Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}+1}(u_{1}^{p_{1}},u_{2}^{p_{2}})}}\right)

\displaystyle\in V\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q_{b_{1}^{\prime}-1+\frac{p_{1}-1}{p_{1}},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(u_{1}^{p_{1}},u_{2}^{p_{2}}),Q_{b_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{p_{1}-1}{p_{1}},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}+1}(u_{1}^{p_{1}},u_{2}^{p_{2}})}}\right),

which by (
96
) is locally bounded if and only if
b_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{p_{1}-1}{p_{1}}\geq 1
and
b_{2}^{\prime}\geq 0
. By applying the same argument for
\partial_{u_{2}}Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(u_{1}^{p_{1}},u_{2}^{p_{2}})
, if follows that
\bm{u}\mapsto Q_{b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(u_{1}^{p_{1}},u_{2}^{p_{2}})
is locally Lipschitz in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
if and only if
b_{j}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{p_{j}}\geq 0
for
j=1,2
. In our case, we have
\frac{1}{p_{j}}=a_{j}-1
. Hence, local Lipschitz-continuity of
\theta_{j,i}
holds if and only if
b_{j}^{\prime}-a_{j}+1\geq 0
for every
b_{j}^{\prime}
appearing in the first and fourth line of (
100
). This however follows from the inequality
b_{j}\geq 2a_{j}-1
, thus concluding the proof.
∎
